L18: ## 1. Executive Summary
L19: ## 1.1. Summary of Regulatory Action
L20: Novo Nordisk originally submitted their Biologic License Application (BLA) for marketing approval of concizumab for routine prophylaxis to prevent or reduce the frequency of bleeding episodes in adult and pediatric patients with hemophilia A (congenital factor VIII deficiency) with FVIII inhibitors and hemophilia B (congenital factor IX deficiency) with FIX inhibitors on August 24, 2022. Concizumab is an anti-tissue factor pathway inhibitor administered by injection. During the initial review cycle of the application, multiple deficiencies were identified which precluded approval. This included concerns about inadequate monitoring for missed doses, need for clear labeling instructions regarding missed doses, need for a Medication Guide, issues regarding the interim concizumab assay proposed for product launch and product quality review issues including facilities inspectional deficiencies. The Applicant received a Complete Response Letter (CRL) on April 24, 2023. The reasons for the CRL are summarized below in Section 2, Regulatory History, of this review.
L21: On June 20, 2024, the Applicant submitted a response to the CRL, addressing the deficiencies listed in the CRL. This resubmission review focuses only on the review of the deficiencies identified in the CRL, updated safety information, labeling and postmarketing commitments. This review is a joint review by Clinical and Clinical Pharmacology.
L22: The Applicant has adequately addressed all deficiencies in the CRL. Each discipline recommends approval, and the signatory authority concurs that the application should be approved. The overall benefit/risk profile is favorable as described in the Benefit-Risk Framework below.
L23: ## 1.2. Benefit-Risk Assessment
L24: ## Benefit-Risk Dimensions
L25: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Analysis of Condition	Hemophilia A (HA) is an X-linked recessive disorder caused by mutations in the FVIII gene which results in a severe deficiency or absence of the clotting protein factor VIII. HA occurs in approximately 1 in 5000 live male births. Patients are typically diagnosed in infancy. Patients are at increased risk for minor and serious bleeding including life threatening spontaneous or traumatic bleeding. Disease manifestations include mucosal bleeding, easy bruising, soft tissue and muscle bleeds, hemarthrosis and excessive bleeding with procedures or trauma. Bleeding can occur in vital organs (including the brain). Adults commonly experience hemophilic arthropathy due to recurrent joint bleeds, which is associated with significant disease morbidity.
Hemophilia B (HB) is an X-linked recessive disorder caused by mutations in the FIX gene which results in a severe deficiency or absence of the clotting protein factor IX. HB occurs in approximately 1 in 30,000 live male births. Deficient factor IX levels result in an increased risk for minor and serious bleeding, including life threatening spontaneous or traumatic bleeding. Disease manifestations of HB are the same as HA described in the text above.
One of the most serious treatment complications of HA and HB is inhibitor development. Approximately 30% of patients with HA and 5-15% of patients HB develop inhibitors to factor replacement. Because bleeding is more difficult to prevent and treat, patients with inhibitors often experience increased disease morbidity and mortality.
Hemophilia A (congenital factor VIII deficiency) with FVIII inhibitors (HAwI) and hemophilia B (congenital factor IX deficiency) with FIX inhibitors (HBwI) are serious chronic diseases with significant morbidity and mortality due to bleeding. Patients with inhibitors have a worse disease course.
L26: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Current Treatment Options	FDA approved bypassing agents are used to prevent or control bleeding in patients with inhibitors. Activated prothrombin complex concentrate (aPCC) is indicated to control and prevent bleeding episodes in patients with HAwI and HBwI. Recombinant activated coagulation factor VII (rFVIIa), is indicated for the treatment of bleeding episodes in patient with HAwI and HBwI. Efficacy of these products can be variable, and they carry a boxed warning for thrombosis. Antibodies to FVIIa can develop in patients with HAwI and HBwI and interfere with the effectiveness of these factor-based therapies.
Alloantibodies or inhibitors develop with exposure to exogenous factor, and block the activity of factor VIII or IX. Inhibitors can make it difficult to stop excessive bleeding as it leads to reduced efficacy of factor replacement.
Patients with HAwI and HBwI may undergo immune ITI to eradicate the inhibitor, but this is only successful in 60-80% of cases in patients with HAwI and 15-30% of patients with HBwI.
Emicizumab (HEMLIBRA) is a bispecific factor IXa and factor X-directed antibody that was FDA approved in 2017 for the indication of routine prophylaxis to prevent or reduce the frequency of bleeding episodes in adult and pediatric patients ages newborn and older with HA or HAwI. Emicizumab is administered by weekly up to every 4 weeks by SC injection. Emicizumab has a boxed warning for thrombotic microangiopathy and thromboembolism associated with use with aPCC.
There is a need for available, safe and effective therapies. Patient with HA and HB can develop inhibitors after exposure to exogenous factor. For patients with HAwI and HBwI, prophylactic treatment options are very limited, in particular for patients with HBwI.
L27: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Benefit	
Pivotal, Phase 3 Study 4311
Study 4311 was a multi-center, open-label, randomized-controlled study in which 52 adult and adolescent patients (27 HAwI, and 25 HBwI), previously treated with on-demand therapy, were randomized 1:2 to arm 1, no prophylaxis (on demand therapy) (N=19) or arm 2, concizumab prophylaxis (N=33) for at least 24 weeks. Arms 3 and 4 included 81 additional patients (53 HAwI and 28 HBwI) treated with concizumab prophylaxis. Any bleeds occurring in the clinical study were treated with the patient's usual bypassing product or factor. Superiority of concizumab PPX (arm 2) over no PPX (arm 1) was confirmed for the primary endpoint of number of treated spontaneous and traumatic bleeding episodes. The annualized bleeding rate (ABR) was calculated as the number of treated spontaneous or traumatic bleeds divided by the number of days in the analysis set, multiplied by 365. The ABR was 1.7 with 95% CI (1.01, 2.87) for patients on concizumab PPX (arm 2) and 11.8 with 95% CI (7.03, 19.86) for patients on no PPX (arm 1). The estimated ABR ratio was 0.14 with 95% CI (0.07, 0.29) corresponding to an 86% reduction in ABR for patients on concizumab PPX (arm 2) compared to no PPX (arm 1). Results were statistically significant and the magnitude of reduction in bleeding rate was clinically meaningful for a patient population at risk for frequent bleeding that is difficult to treat. While there is a theoretical concern that patients may alter their behavior due to knowledge of their treatment assignment, the highly statistically significant efficacy result provides reassurance of treatment effect.
Key secondary endpoints of change in SF-36v2 bodily pain and change in SF-36v2 physical functioning were not statistically significant.
Prophylactic treatment with daily concizumab is expected to reduce spontaneous and traumatic bleeding episodes in patients with HAwI and HBwI. This was evident by the reduction in the number of bleeding events in patients treated with concizumab prophylaxis compared to on demand therapy. Concizumab will fulfill an unmet need in that there are no non-factor therapies currently approved to reduce bleeding in patients with HBwI.
L28: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
In a descriptive analysis, 94.7% of patients in arm 1 (no PPX) had a treated bleed or discontinued before week 24 compared to 48.5% in arm 2 (concizumab PPX).
Supportive, Phase 2 Study 4310
Study 4310 was a supportive, multicenter, open-label, randomized-controlled study in which 26 patients (16 HAwI patients, 10 HBwI) patients were randomized 2:1 to concizumab PPX (n=17) and eptacog alfa (rFVIIa) on demand (N=9) for at least 24 weeks. The primary endpoint the number of bleeding episodes during at least 24 weeks from treatment onset. The ABR was significantly lower in the concizumab PPX arm compared to the eptacog arm. The estimated ABR during the entire treatment period was also lower in the concizumab arm compared to the eptacog alfa arm: 5.4 (95% CI: 3.8, 7.6) and 20.6 (95% CI: 13.7, 30.8), respectively.

Risk and Risk Management	
Overall Safety Findings
The review of safety focused on Study 4311, which included 127 patients with HAwI and HBwI exposed to concizumab, including 33 patients randomized to concizumab PPX (arm 2) compared to 19 patients randomized to no PPX (arm 1) in the main treatment period. The safety analysis was further supported by Study 4310.
TEAEs were reported in 60% of patients in concizumab PPX arm 2 compared to 42% patients in the on-demand arm 1 in the randomized main treatment portion of study 4311. The most common TEAE in the main treatment period and occurred in a higher proportion of patients in the concizumab arm compared to no PPX was local administration reactions, which occurred in 18% of patients in the concizumab arm (arm 2) compared to 0% in the on-
Several risks have been identified that will be included in the Warnings and Precautions section of the USPI, this includes risk of thrombosis, hypersensitivity reactions, and increased laboratory values of fibrin D-dimer and prothrombin fragment 1+2. Local injection site reaction was the most common AR.
Patients with prolonged subtherapeutic levels (<200ng/mL) of concizumab are at increased risk of bleeding, which can be serious and life-threatening. Adherence to the daily SC dosing regimen of concizumab is critical to
L29: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
demand therapy arm (arm 1). The most common adverse reactions (\ge 5\%) also includes urticaria.
Thrombosis
In total, four patients across clinical trials experienced serious events of thrombosis, including arterial and venous thrombosis, after receiving concizumab, compared to no patients in the control group. Notable characteristics were additional risk factors for thrombosis (i.e., smoking, obesity and HTN), receiving factor product or a bypassing agent within 24 hours of the thrombotic event and elevated concizumab levels (>4000\text{ng/mL}).
The Applicant had modified the concizumab dosing regimen during the clinical development to target a concizumab level between 200\text{-}4000\text{ng/mL}, to prevent bleeding and thrombosis. In addition, the Applicant included a number of mitigation strategies, including using the lowest labeled dose of factor or bypassing agent.
Risk of Bleeding with Subtherapeutic Concizumab Levels.
Across clinical trials, the majority of patients with subtherapeutic concizumab levels (<200\text{ ng/mL}) had no bleeding or minor bleeding although, 6 (1.9%) patients had serious bleeding events and 2 (0.6%) patients died with subtherapeutic levels.
Large PK fluctuations were noted in clinical studies, this was in part due to issues with non-compliance. PK analysis demonstrates missed doses of concizumab can result in subtherapeutic levels within a few days, further, once the patient restarts administration it may take several days to become therapeutic.
prevent prolonged subtherapeutic levels. Therefore, the importance of adherence to daily dosing will be stressed in labeling, a Medication Guide and education materials.
In addition, a plan for missed concizumab doses is described in labeling.
Achieving concizumab levels >200\text{ng/mL} is key to the therapeutic effect of concizumab and levels <4000\text{ng/mL} is critical to decrease the risk of thrombosis. A companion diagnostic concizumab assay is necessary for effective and safe use of this product, however due to the unmet need, concizumab will be approved but not concurrently with the proposed companion diagnostic, which is under review with CDRH. In the interim, an assay (b) (4) will be made available by the Applicant to prescribers to guide dosing and management. Development of a companion diagnostic assay will be requested in a PMC.

L38: ## Conclusions Regarding Benefit-Risk
L39: - HAWL and HBWL are rare and serious diseases, associated with significant morbidity and mortality due to a risk of frequent and severe bleeding. Prophylactic factor-based treatment is considered standard of care to prevent bleeding related complications. However, antibodies to FVIIa can develop in patients and interfere with the effectiveness of these factor-based therapies. There is an unmet medical need for prophylactic non-factor therapy options for patients with HAWL and HBWL.
L40: Concizumab is an anti-tissue factor pathway inhibitor which enhances FXa production during the initiation phase of coagulation which leads to improved thrombin generation and clot formation to achieve hemostasis.
L41: The efficacy of concizumab was based on a single, adequate and well controlled trial, study 4311. Study 4311 compared ABR "on demand" treatment with a bypassing agent, eptacog alfa or activated prothrombin complex concentrate, administered at the time of bleeding with prophylaxis concizumab treatment. The efficacy results demonstrated a statistically significant reduction in median ABR for the prophylaxis concizumab treatment group when compared with the "on demand" treatment group. Confirmatory evidence included study results from study 4310, which also demonstrated a large reduction in ABR in the group on concizumab prophylaxis and mechanistic support.
L42: The safety of concizumab was assessed in the randomized controlled portion of study 4311, along with the entire safety database in the concizumab hemophilia development plan including 320 patients with HAWL, HBWL, HA and HB. Common adverse reactions included injection site reactions and urticaria. Similar to other treatments for hemophilia, concizumab carries an increased risk of thromboembolic events. The risk of thrombosis can be decreased with adjusting the concizumab maintenance dose to the concizumab concentration target range as described in the USPI and following blood management guidelines. We will continue to assess the risk of thrombosis with postmarketing enhanced pharmacovigilance. Concizumab also carries an increased risk of serious hypersensitivity reactions.
L43: The benefit/risk profile of concizumab is now favorable, as the Applicant has adequately addressed deficiencies in the CRL, including guidance on missed doses, emphasized the importance of drug compliance in labeling, and resolved issues related to product quality and facility inspections. At this time, there is no FDA-cleared assay to measure concizumab levels, which are used to guide safe and effective dosing. While the Applicant completes development of a companion diagnostic assay (see post-marketing commitment in Section 8) an interim assay will be used to assess concizumab levels. The unapproved interim assay was reviewed by the Agency and determined to be adequate for use for this purpose.
L44: ## 2. Regulatory History
L45: Novo Nordisk originally submitted their BLA for routine prophylaxis to prevent or reduce the frequency of bleeding episodes in adult and pediatric patients with hemophilia A (congenital factor VIII deficiency) with FVIII inhibitors and hemophilia B (congenital factor IX deficiency) with FIX inhibitors on August 24, 2022. The Applicant received a CRL on April 24, 2023. The reasons for the CRL are briefly summarized below, see finalized CRL in DARRTS for further details.
L46: ## Deficiencies identified in the CRL:
L47: **Clinical and Clinical Pharmacology:** At the target exposure range of concizumab, target-mediated drug disposition (TMDD) plays a dominant role in concizumab clearance, leading to rapid drug elimination and a relatively shorter half-life at the proposed doses of 0.15 - 0.25 mg/kg. As a result, concizumab concentrations can decline steeply to subtherapeutic levels with missed doses and it may take several days of consecutive dosing to gradually return to target concentrations of &gt;200 ng/mL. Therefore, a plan for dosing after one or more missed doses is required, including an additional concizumab pharmacokinetics (PK) monitoring and/or a repeat loading dose. In addition, the importance of adherence to daily dosing and maintenance of therapeutic concizumab levels should be stressed in a Medication Guide, labeling and educational materials.
L48: **Device:** Four weeks after initiating concizumab prophylaxis, a concizumab-mtci plasma concentration will be measured by Concizumab Enzyme-Linked Immunosorbent Assay (ELISA) for dose optimization purposes. Based on the ELISA assay results, the dose of concizumab may be modified in order to maintain the target range. The Applicant's interim assay to monitor concizumab concentration was inadequate for a multitude of reasons outlined in the CRL.
L49: **Product Quality:** [REDACTED] (b) (4) are present in the concizumab drug product. The Applicant provided insufficient information and data provided to address the risk of [REDACTED] (b) (4) on safety and efficacy and the control strategy for [REDACTED] (b) (4) was inadequate to ensure product quality, safety, and efficacy. Other deficiencies were identified including inadequate cell bank stability protocols, inadequate reference material stability protocols, inadequate drug substance (DS) and drug product (DP) release and stability specifications, and inadequate DS and DP post-approval annual stability protocols.
L50: **Facility Inspection:** Following inspection of [REDACTED] (b) (4) manufacturing facility listed in this application, FDA conveyed deficiencies to the representative of the facility.
L51: ## Type A Meeting to Discuss Deficiencies in CRL:
L52: On June 23, 2023, a Type A in-person meeting was held to discuss the deficiencies in the CRL
L53: and potential path forward. Key discussion points from the meeting are listed below, see finalized meeting minutes in DARRTS for further details.
L54: - The Applicant proposed guidance for resumed dosing after missed concizumab doses. The rationale of this plan was to ensure that patients who have missed concizumab daily injection at the maintenance dose stage could reach steady-state concizumab exposure levels as quickly as possible upon resumed dosing without exceeding steady-state levels. Overall, the Agency commented that the proposal was reasonable, however emphasized that the USPI and Medication Guide should further stress the importance of adherence to daily dosing and the consequence of missed doses.
- The Applicant proposed to include an additional concizumab plasma concentration level to be performed 8 weeks after initiation of maintenance dosing. The Agency commented that the proposal was reasonable, but additional clarification should be provided on what to do with the results of the level.
- The Agency did not agree with the Applicant's data package, characterization study and control strategy was sufficient to address concerns of safety, efficacy and product quality regarding [REDACTED] (b) (4) in the drug product. The Agency advised the Applicant to submit a separate meeting request to discuss this issue further.
- The Agency stated that the Applicant's proposed additions to the release and shelf-life specifications, post-approval stability protocol for on-going stability in the assembled pen-injector, along with the proposal to provide analytical produce and validation of analytical procedure was reasonable.
- The Agency clarified that the facility evaluation will be performed during the resubmission review cycle.
- The Agency agreed with the proposed content of the safety update.
- The Agency stated that the [REDACTED] (b) (4) concizumab-ELISA remains a deficiency as described in the CRL.
L55: ## Type B Meeting for [REDACTED] (b) (4)
L56: On April 15, 2024, a Type B videoconference was held to discuss the Applicant's proposed control strategy for [REDACTED] (b) (4) to be included in the resubmission of the BLA and gain agreement on the implementation plan for CAPA based on the identified main root cause for formation of visible particles. Key responses from the meeting are listed below, see finalized meeting minutes in DARRTS for further details.
L57: - The Agency did not agree with the Applicant's proposed drug product control strategy and stated it was not possible to determine the clinical impact from [REDACTED] (b) (4) in the concizumab drug product. In a post-meeting comment, the Agency emphasized given the transient nature of the particles and inability to determine if a subject received drug with particles, it will be very challenging to understand the safety profile and efficacy impact of [REDACTED] (b) (4).
- The Applicant stated the main root cause for formation of [REDACTED] (b) (4) is identified [REDACTED] (b) (4). In addition, the
L58: Applicant planned to include a comparability protocol detailing the post-approval change in the resubmission.
L59: ## 3. Deficiencies Identified in the CRL
L60: ## 3.1. Clinical and Clinical Pharmacology
L61: To address the potential for significant fluctuations in concizumab concentration levels, which can lead to subtherapeutic levels in the setting of missed doses and thus increasing the risk of serious bleeding events, the Applicant proposed several mitigation strategies, including (1) guidance on missed doses, (2) guidance on additional concizumab measurements, and (3) additional education and guidance to emphasize the importance of adherence to the prescribed dosing regimen.
L62: ## Guidance on missed doses when a patient has forgotten or neglected to take their once-daily maintenance dose
L63: The Applicant has proposed the following plan to be included in the USPI for concizumab:
L64: - 1 missed dose: Resume once-daily treatment at the maintenance dose level
- 2 to 6 missed doses: Resume treatment with a double dose followed by once-daily treatment at the maintenance dose level
- 7 or more missed doses: Physician should be contacted, and a new loading dose should be considered
L65: The above proposal was based on population PK modelling. The final population PK model, reviewed earlier by the Agency with original BLA submission, was used to simulate individual concentration-time profiles for 10,000 subjects with different numbers of missed doses followed by treatment being resumed with either a single dose, a double dose or a new loading dose. The maintenance dose setting was implemented as a dose increase to 0.25 mg/kg or a decrease to 0.15 mg/kg 6 weeks after first dose, based on Ctrough values 4 weeks after first dose. In the simulations, exposure was at steady state at the time of the first missed dose and the body-weight distribution was similar to trial 4311.
L66: In the setting of 1 missed dose, resumption of treatment at the same maintenance dose level on the following day with continued once-daily maintenance dose is shown to result in patients quickly reaching and maintaining the steady state levels (Figure 1).
L67: **Figure 1:** Simulation of concizumab plasma concentration when treatment is resumed with a single maintenance dose followed by once-daily maintenance dose after 1 missed dose.
L68: In the setting of 2 to 6 missed doses, simulations show that doubling the maintenance dose following missed dose(s) is able to bring concizumab exposures above 200 ng/mL reasonably quickly and achieve steady state exposures with continued once-daily maintenance doses (Figure 2). Use of a loading dose to resume treatment in the scenario of 2 to 6 missed doses is not appropriate because the resulting exposures is expected to exceed the prior steady state levels.
L69: **Figure 2:** Simulation of concizumab plasma concentration when treatment is resumed with a double maintenance dose followed by once-daily maintenance dose after 2 (left) or 6 (right) missed doses.
L70: In the setting of 7 or more missed doses, a loading dose of 1 mg/kg followed by once-daily maintenance doses is more appropriate as it results in concizumab exposures achieving steady state levels as quickly as possible without exceeding the prior steady state levels (Figure 3).
L71: **Figure 3: Simulation of concizumab plasma concentration when treatment is resumed with a loading dose followed by once-daily maintenance dose after 7 missed doses.**
L72: ## Guidance on Concizumab Measurements
L73: Four weeks after initiation of concizumab, prescribers are advised to check concizumab-mtci plasma concentration by Concizumab ELISA prior to administration of next scheduled dose for dose optimization. Based on the results the concizumab dose may be adjusted. In order to further monitor patient compliance, Novo Nordisk proposed to add the following guidance for additional monitoring of concizumab plasma concentration to the USPI:
L74: *Based on the patient's condition, additional measurements of concizumab-mtci plasma concentration (b) (4) be taken (b) (4) so long as the patient has been on the same maintenance dose for 8 weeks of treatment to ensure steady-state plasma concentration.*
L75: The timing of the additional measurement i.e., 8 weeks beyond the dose adjustment period is reasonable, as it ensures achievement of new steady state levels in 90% of patients who underwent dose-adjustment. The Agency clarified further in the USPI that additional measurements of concizumab-mtci plasma concentration can be used to assess compliance.
L76: ## Guidance and Education on Compliance
L77: In order to stress the importance of adherence to daily dosing during the initial dosing period for appropriate maintenance dose setting, Novo Nordisk further proposed including the following guidance in the USPI and the corresponding instructions to be included in the Medication Guide:
L78: *Adherence to daily dosing of Alhemo is important to maintain protection against bleeding. This is especially important during the initial 4 weeks of treatment to ensure a correct maintenance dose is established. Patients who miss a dose during the initial 4-week period should inform their healthcare professional and resume once-daily dosing at the initial 0.2 mg/kg dose level.*
L79: *Advise patients on the importance of adherence to daily dosing and to speak with their healthcare provider before discontinuing treatment with Alhemo as patients who are not*
L80: Clinical Review
BLA 761315
Alhemo (concizumab)
L81: *adhering to daily dosing or stop treatment with Alhemo may no longer be protected against bleeding.*
L82: The Medication Guide will replace the Patient Product Insert (PPI) and text will be added on the carton with directions regarding dispensing the Medication Guide to each patient. The Medication Guide will include the following statement:
L83: *It is important to follow the daily dosing schedule to stay protected against bleeding. Take ALHEMO exactly as prescribed by your healthcare provider.*
L84: In addition, Novo Nordisk commits to developing educational material conveying similar messages based on the final approved labeling text.
L85: ## Summary
L86: The Applicant has adequately addressed the clinical and clinical pharmacology concerns regarding missed doses and strategies to monitor and promote patient compliance. The Agency agrees with the additional guidance on missed doses to be included in labeling, the recommendation for an additional concizumab level anytime beyond 8 weeks post-dose adjustment and guidance along with education materials to stress the importance of adherence to the prescribed dosing regimen.
L87: ## 3.2. Device
L88: ## Concizumab Enzyme Linked Immunosorbent Assay (ELISA) and Intended Use:
L89: (b) (4)
L90: -
L91: - It is intended for concizumab concentration measurements in human 3.2% citrated plasma samples from patients after 4 weeks from the initiation of treatment for maintenance dose adjustment decision.
- It will be used in an interim period to bridge the time gap from concizumab BLA approval until a (b) (4) PMA-approved concizumab ELISA companion diagnostic is available.
L92: ## Complete Response Letter (CRL) Issue 2: Cross-validation between concizumab ELISA (b) (4) vs (b) (4) clinical trial assay (CTA)
L93: *Include sufficient number of native patient samples and samples that cover a range that are pertinent to dose adjustment decisions.*
L94: ## Applicant's Response:
L95: The Applicant repeated the cross-validation study using 120 individual patient samples (i.e., native samples from Phase 3 clinical trials, NN7415-4311 and NN7415-4307). The sample material was residual PK assay samples which were left-over from PK samples derived from clinical trial visits other than dose decision visit. The dose decision visit samples are being used for (b) (4) cross-validation and the proposed plan was accepted by the (b) (4) Agency.
L96: (b) (4)
L97: ## **Reviewer's Assessment:**
L98: Reference is made to the *ICH M10 Bioanalytical Method Validation and Sample Analysis guidance*. Cross validation should be assessed by measuring the same set of QCs (low, medium, and high) at least in triplicate and study samples (if available) that span the study sample concentration range (n \ge 30) with both methods, or in both laboratories. The performance of calibration standards (CSs) and quality controls (QCs) for each method was not reported for the cross validation. However, it was noted that the performance of the CSs and QCs of the concizumab ELISA at [REDACTED] and concizumab ELISA CTA at [REDACTED] were evaluated as a part of their own method validation and the validation results were found to be acceptable.
L99: (b) (4)
L100: Overall, the Applicant's response regarding cross validation is acceptable.

L129: ## 4. Review of Safety Update
L130: ## 4.1. Safety Review Approach
L131: As requested in the CRL, the Applicant submitted a Safety Update Report with this resubmission.
L132: The updated safety database for trials supporting the safety analysis of concizumab consists of multiple-dose trials (i.e., 4307, 4255, 4311, 4310 and 4159) (see Table 12 in the Appendix for an overview of the clinical trials). The Applicant's figure below shows that 320 patients total are included in the trials in patients with hemophilia before and/or after the treatment pause. Since the initial HAwI and HBwI application, results from a new (56-week) cut-off for phase 3 trial 4311 (HAwI and HBwI) and from two cut-offs (confirmatory analysis cut-off (CACO) and the 56-week cut-off) for phase 3 trial 4307 (HA and HB) were reviewed. The resubmission data cutoff date was March 31, 2024. Compassionate use data was also submitted from 27 patients.
L133: Figure 5. Safety Database Pool by Trial
L134: Notes: a16 patients with HA from trial 4255 were transferred to trial 4307 (cross-reference: ADSL.sas7bdat [Trial 4307]) and 21 patients from trial 4310 were transferred to trial 4311 (Trial 4311 PACO [M5.3.5.1], Table 10-1 [arm 3]). bPatients randomised to on demand treatment who were exposed to at least one dose of concizumab following the main part of the trial, contributed to the pooled safety evaluation. cThe 56-week cut-off was defined as when all patients in arms 2, 3 and 4 had completed visit 13a (or permanently discontinued treatment). dPatients randomised to on-demand treatment in the main part, who have been exposed to at least one dose of concizumab in the extension part of the trial, contribute to the pooled safety evaluation. Further details on exposure to concizumab are available in Section 1.2.

L153: ## 4.3.1. Issues Regarding Data Integrity and Submission Quality

For the safety update, no issues identified regarding data integrity and submission quality.
L155: ## 4.3.2. Categorization of Adverse Events
L156: Adverse events were coded as Medical Dictionary for Regulatory Activities (MedDRA) preferred terms (version 17.0 to 26.0). The Applicant used version 26.0 of MedDRA for the outputs based on the pooled data. The majority of adverse events were analyzed using standardized MedDRA queries. Hypersensitivity was analyzed using FDA Medical Query.
L157: ## 4.4. Safety Results
L158: ## 4.4.1. Deaths
L159: In the original safety pool, there were 4 patient deaths including events of road traffic accident, COVID-19, alcoholic coma and intracranial hemorrhage. The event of intracranial hemorrhage occurred in an adolescent with subtherapeutic concizumab levels (see finalized Integrated Review dated April 24, 2023, for further details).
L160: One new death in a patient with HA (treated on study 4307) was reported in the Safety Update Report. Patient (b) (6), was a 54-year-old male, with HA treated with concizumab PPX who was reported to have died due to intra-abdominal hemorrhage. In Trial 4307, on study day 276, the patient was found dead at home. The exact event onset date remains unknown. The cause of death based on autopsy was reported bleeding to death by diffuse intra-abdominal bleed of 1.7 L of blood without a clearly localizable bleeding source. The event was judged by the investigator as possibly related to concizumab, with alternative causality reported as “underlying disease severe hemophilia A”.
L161: This patient was treated with concizumab PPX at a dose of 0.15 mg/kg in trial 4255 for approximately 2 years and 8 months. The patient was then enrolled in Trial 4307 and was treated with concizumab PPX (0.25 mg/kg) for approximately 8 months prior to the onset of the fatal event. There were no bleeding events reported for the patient in either trial up until the fatal event. Of note, the patient had an ongoing concomitant illness of hypertension treated with bisoprolol. Blood pressure measurements performed at the last 5 visits before event onset were reported to have from around 120-140/80 mmHg to around 155-170 /100-110mmHg.
L162: An Information Request was sent to the Applicant to provide additional information regarding concizumab level(s) and compliance. The Applicant reported that all concizumab plasma concentration measurements performed for the patient after the maintenance dose setting were below 200 ng/mL. The concizumab plasma concentration gradually increased from approximately 70 ng/mL to fluctuating between approximately 90 and 175 ng/mL from week 8 up until the last measurement at week 32. There was seemingly only a minor effect of the maintenance dose increase to 0.25 mg/kg.
L163: Despite the low plasma concizumab levels, free TFPI in plasma was well-suppressed at all visits where measured. The patient did not test positive for anti-concizumab antibodies (ADAs) at any visit. No protocol deviations related to missed doses or compliance issues were registered for the patient.
L164: *Reviewer comment: The cause for this patient's intra-abdominal bleeding event is not clear. Although concizumab levels were consistently below the recommended levels of 200ng/mL the patient did not have other bleeding events. Notably, there was no evidence of antidrug antibodies. As described in the original BLA review, with low concentration of concizumab (&lt;200ng/mL) patients are at risk for serious and life-threatening bleeding events. In fact, there was one other case of a fatal bleed described in the original review; however, this patient/caregiver may have had compliance issues. Per the Applicant the other patient who died due to intra-abdominal hemorrhage was compliant, but uncertainty remains.*
L165: *To minimize risk of serious and life-threatening bleeding with subtherapeutic concizumab levels, the label should include instructions to advise prescribers/patients to consider alternative*
L166: *therapies if the therapeutic range for concizumab cannot be achieved. Additional concizumab level monitoring to assess for subtherapeutic levels needs to be clearly stated in the label.*
L167: ## 4.4.2 Serious Adverse Events

Similar proportions of patients reported SAEs in the updated (n=320 patient) and the initial safety pool (n=185 patient), 13.0% and 15.9%, respectively, and the rate was 0.1 events per PYE in both pools. Among patients with HAwl and HBwl there were 26/131 (19.8%) who reported SAEs. The table below shows that 10 patients with SAEs required discontinuation of the study drug. Cases with thrombotic adverse events of special interest were previously discussed in the Integrated Review final signature date April 24, 2023.

L184: ## 4.4.2. Dropouts and/or Discontinuations Due to Adverse Effects
L185: In the updated total safety pool, 60 (18.8%) patients who temporarily interrupted treatment or permanently discontinued therapy compared to 21 (16.9% patients originally reported in study 4311 and 4310). There were 10 (3.1%) patients who permanently discontinued study drug. AEs leading to study drug discontinuation were all reported in 1 patient each, including, intra-abdominal hemorrhage, procedural complication, acute myocardial infarction, cardiac failure, deep vein thrombosis, injection site pain, cardiomyopathy, renal infarct, hypersensitivity and COVID-19 infection. Patients with permanent study drug discontinuation ranged in age from 13-67 years. The duration of therapy prior to discontinuation ranged from 10-205 days. Of note, protocols required temporary treatment discontinuation for COVID-19 infection.

L189: Clinical reviewer comment: Patient (b) (6) had a serious event of intra-abdominal bleeding. The patient was a 43-year-old male with HB who was treated with concizumab on study 4307. On day 2 of therapy the patient was admitted to hospital due to right upper quadrant abdominal pain. The patient underwent a CT scan of the abdomen and chest which showed blood in the area of the spleen and liver, and a lung mass, respectively. The lung mass was biopsied which was subsequently shown to have lymphocytic changes but no malignancy. The patient's concizumab therapy was discontinued at the time of the event. Although abdominal bleeding is not a common site for bleeding in patients with hemophilia it has been reported. This serious bleeding event is likely due to the underlying disease as the patient had just initiated concizumab therapy.
L190: The majority of events leading to treatment discontinuation are included in labeling with mitigation strategies (i.e., bleeding due to subtherapeutic levels, thrombosis, and hypersensitivity reaction).

L197: ## 4.4.4. Adverse Events of Special Interest
L198: ## 1. Thromboembolic events
L199: In the Safety Update Report, 4 (1.3%) patients experienced SAEs of thromboembolic events, 3 out the 4 patients were reported in the original review. The one new case of serious thromboembolic event (cerebral infarction) occurred in a 63 year-old male with HA. The patient had a medical history of hypertension since 2013 and was treated with 2 anti-hypertensive medications. The patient was previously enrolled in the phase 2 trial 4255 and had more than 5 years of concizumab exposure up until onset of the SAE, including exposure before and after the treatment pause in trial 4307. On the day of event onset, the patient experienced visual disturbance, facial weakness, ataxia and slurred speech. The patient was diagnosed with an ischemic stroke. Different scans, MRI brain scans, were performed, and a small recent infarction was detected in 2 different areas (left hemipons and corona radiata). According to the investigator, the MRI images of the patient's brain as well as the CT scans taken after event onset were shared with a specialist (neuro-radiologist) to get a second opinion. According to the specialist, the specific lesions (perforating arteries) found on the scans are most seen in patients with a medical history of hypertension. The patient discontinued concizumab treatment permanently.
L200: *Clinical reviewer comment: Thrombosis is a serious adverse reaction associated with concizumab, likely due to exaggerated pharmacologic effect and will be included in the Warnings and Precautions section of the USPI. While providing specific guidance for use of the lowest labeled dose of factor product or bypassing agent while on concizumab PPX and a concizumab dosing regimen which targets concizumab levels &lt;4000ng/mL, this risk remains, as evident by the event of cerebral infarction. Risk factors for thromboembolism further increase the risk of thrombosis and may include the use of high and/or frequent doses of breakthrough bleed treatments (factor products or bypassing agents) or conditions in which tissue factor is overexpressed (e.g., atherosclerotic disease, crush injury, cancer, disseminated intravascular coagulation, thrombotic microangiopathy, or septicemia). Thromboembolic events occurred in patients with multiple risk factors for thromboembolism, including the use of high doses or prolonged treatment with factor product or bypassing agent (2 of 4 events). Enhanced pharmacovigilance is recommended to further monitor the risk of thrombosis.*
L201: ## 2. Hypersensitivity reactions
L202: In the Safety Update Report, the search for FDA Medical Query (FMQ) for hypersensitivity reactions was reported in 30 (9.4%) patients totaling 55 events (0.1 events per PYE). The most common terms reported in \ge 4 (approximately \ge 1\%) patients were 'hypersensitivity', 'injection site reaction', and 'pruritis'. The reviewer's table below shows the proportion of patients with hypersensitivity reactions reported in
L203: ≥ 4 patients using the FMQ search by disease subtype. One event of serious hypersensitivity reaction (anaphylaxis) was reported in the original review, no new events of anaphylaxis were reported. No events were fatal. Two events led to permanent treatment discontinuation. All but one event was characterized mild-moderate severity.

L213: *Clinical reviewer comment: The plasma concentration of concizumab is positively correlated with fibrin D-dimer and prothrombin fragments 1.2 indicating a hemostatic effect of concizumab. For patients taking concizumab, these coagulation biomarkers may not be reliable predictive markers for clinical decision-making with suspicion of*
L214: *thrombosis such as deep vein thrombosis (DVT) and pulmonary embolism (PE), therefore this risk will be included in the Warnings and Precautions section of the USPI.*
L215: ## 5. Advisory Committee Meeting and Other External Consultations
L216: An advisory committee (AC) meeting was not convened to discuss this application. No issues were identified that would have benefitted from a public discussion with external experts.
L217: ## 6. Labeling Recommendations
L218: ## 6.1. Prescription Drug Labeling
L219: Alhemo will be approved with Prescribing Information, Medication Guide, and 3 Instructions for Use (one per pen strength).
L220: CDER chose to approve concizumab without its companion diagnostic given the unmet need for patients with hemophilia with inhibitors. A postmarketing commitment is recommended for the development of the companion diagnostic. The label will state there are no FDA authorized tests. This is the same approach we have consistently used when CDER chooses to approve a drug without its companion diagnostic. The Center for Devices and Radiological Health (CDRH) agreed with CDER's approach and advised on labeling related to the use of an unauthorized concizumab-ELISA.
L221: Summary of Significant Labeling Changes (High Level Changes)
Section	Proposed Labeling	Approved Labeling
1 Indications and Usage	
The Applicant proposed the following indication statement: Alhemo is a tissue factor pathway inhibitor (TFPI) antagonist indicated for routine prophylaxis to prevent or reduce the frequency of bleeding episodes in adult and pediatric patients
hemophilia A (congenital factor VIII deficiency) with FVIII inhibitors
hemophilia B (congenital factor IX deficiency) with FIX inhibitors
The established pharmacologic class (EPC) was edited to replace "[REDACTED] (b) (4)". The EPC for this drug is "tissue factor pathway inhibitor antagonist.
The Agency added that the indication is for pediatric patients at least 12 years of age, as this is the population included in clinical trials.

2 Dosage and Administration	The Applicant proposed guidance on dosing of concizumab.	Additional guidance on monitoring for subtherapeutic levels was added. Additional language added to stress the importance of adherence.
L222: Summary of Significant Labeling Changes (High Level Changes)
Section	Proposed Labeling	Approved Labeling
Text added to provide guidance to prescribers on how to change to Alhemo from other hemostatic products.
Revised text in 2.4 to clarify that self-administration may occur only after appropriate training.
Added required language regarding inspection for particulate matter for parenteral drug products.

4 Contraindications	Applicant proposed to contraindicate in patients with known hypersensitivity to Alhemo or its components.	Revised to include those with a history of known serious hypersensitivity to Alhemo or its components or the inactive ingredients.
5 Warnings and Precautions	Applicant proposed Warnings for thromboembolic events and hypersensitivity reactions.	
Revised the order to list the most clinically important risk first (thromboembolic events).
Overall changed wording to active voice.
Thromboembolic events (TE)- added additional description including the proportion of patients who experienced a TE and noted that cases occurred in patients with multiple risk factors for thromboembolism, including the use of high doses or prolonged treatment with factor product or bypassing agent (2 of 4 events).
Hypersensitivity reactions- added additional description of the symptoms that were included in hypersensitivity reactions and the case of anaphylaxis.
The Agency requested to add "Increased Laboratory Values of Fibrin D dimer and Prothrombin Fragment 1+2" as a Warning, as for patients taking concizumab, these coagulation biomarkers may not be reliable predictive markers for clinical decision-making with suspicion of thrombosis such as deep vein thrombosis (DVT)

L225: ## 7. Risk Evaluation and Mitigation Strategies (REMS)
L226: Based on the available data, a risk evaluation and mitigation strategy is not necessary to ensure the benefits of concizumab in patients with HAwI and HBwI outweigh the risks.
L227: ## 8. Postmarketing Requirements and Commitments
L228: **PMC 4761-1:** Conduct adequate analytical and clinical validation testing to establish and support labeling of an FDA cleared or approved in vitro diagnostic device to accurately and reliably detect concizumab concentration in human 3.2% citrated plasma that is safe and effective for concizumab dose adjustment decisions for Hemophilia A and B patients (with and without inhibitors) after 4 weeks from the initiation of treatment with concizumab. The results of the validation studies are intended to inform product labeling.

L278: ## I. Executive Summary
L279: ## 1. Summary of Regulatory Action
L280: On August 24, 2022, Novo Nordisk submitted BLA 761315 for concizumab for the following indications: ALHEMO® is a tissue factor pathway inhibitor (TFPI)-directed antibody indicated for routine prophylaxis to prevent or reduce the frequency of bleeding episodes in adult and pediatric patients with hemophilia A (congenital factor VIII deficiency) with FVIII inhibitors (HAWI) and hemophilia B (congenital factor IX deficiency) with coagulation factor IX (FIX) inhibitors (HBWI). This application was reviewed by a multidisciplinary team. This application was given priority review because the proposed indication would have addressed an unmet medical need of a prophylaxis treatment for patients with HBWI.
L281: In 2018 and 2019, concizumab received orphan designation for hemophilia A (HA) and hemophilia B (HB), and in 2019 received Breakthrough Therapy designation for HBWI.
L282: In clinical studies, concizumab prophylaxis use demonstrated an improvement in the annualized bleeding rate (ABR) compared to on demand treatment using a blood product approved for the on-demand treatment of bleeding events for patients with HA and HB. ABR endpoint has been used for product approval for decades for products to treat bleeding events due to hemophilia.
L283: The application has multiple deficiencies including concern about inadequate monitoring for missed doses, need for clear labeling instructions regarding missed doses, need for a Medication Guide, the interim assay proposed for product launch and product quality review issues including facilities inspectional deficiencies which preclude approval.
L284: ## 2. Benefit-Risk Assessment
L285: ## 2.1. Benefit-Risk Framework
L286: Table 2. Benefit-Risk Framework
L287: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Analysis of condition	HA is an X-linked recessive disorder caused by mutations in the FVIII gene which results in a severe deficiency or absence of the clotting protein factor VIII. HA occurs in approximately 1 in 5000 live male births. Patients are typically diagnosed in infancy. Patients are at increased risk for minor and serious bleeding including life threatening spontaneous or traumatic bleeding. Disease manifestations include mucosal bleeding, easy bruising, soft tissue and muscle bleeds, hemarthrosis and excessive bleeding with procedures or trauma. Bleeding can occur in vital organs (including the brain). Adults commonly experience hemophilic arthropathy due to recurrent joint bleeds, which is associated with significant disease morbidity.
HB is an X-linked recessive disorder caused by mutations in the FIX gene which results in a severe deficiency or absence of the clotting protein factor IX. HB occurs in approximately 1 in 30,000 live male births. Deficient factor IX levels results in an increased risk for minor and serious bleeding, including life threatening spontaneous or traumatic bleeding. Disease manifestations of HB are the same as HA described in the text above.
One of the most serious complications of HA and HB is inhibitor development. Approximately 30% of patients with HA and 5-15% of patients HB develop inhibitors. Because bleeding is more difficult to prevent and treat, patients with inhibitors often experience increased disease morbidity and mortality.
Hemophilia A (congenital factor VIII deficiency) with FVIII inhibitors (HAwI) and hemophilia B (congenital factor IX deficiency) with FIX inhibitors (HBwI) are serious chronic diseases with significant morbidity and mortality due to bleeding. Patients with inhibitors have a worse disease course.
L288: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Current treatment options	FDA approved bypassing agents are used to prevent or control bleeding in patients with inhibitors. Activated prothrombin complex concentrate (aPCC) is indicated to control and prevent bleeding episodes in patients with HAwI and HBwI. Recombinant activated coagulation factor VII (rFVIIa), is indicated for the treatment of bleeding episodes in patient with HAwI and HBwI. Efficacy of these products can be variable, and they carry a boxed warning for thrombosis.
Alloantibodies or inhibitors develop with exposure to exogenous factor, and block the activity of factor VIII or IX. Inhibitors can make it difficult to stop excessive bleeding as it leads to reduced efficacy of factor replacement.
Patients with HAwI and HBwI may undergo immune ITI to eradicate the inhibitor, but this is only successful in 60-80% of cases in patients with HAwI and 15-30% of patients with HBwI.
Emicizumab (HEMLIBRA) is a bispecific factor IXa and factor X-directed antibody that was FDA approved in 2017 for the indication of routine prophylaxis to prevent or reduce the frequency of bleeding episodes in adult and pediatric patients ages newborn and older with HA or HAwI. Emicizumab is administered by weekly up to every 4 weeks by SC injection. Emicizumab has a boxed warning for thrombotic microangiopathy and thromboembolism associated with use with aPCC.
There is a need for available, safe and effective therapies. Patient with HA and HB can develop inhibitors after exposure to exogenous factor. For patients with HAwI and HBwI, prophylactic treatment options are very limited, in particular for patients with HBwI.
Benefit	
Pivotal, Phase 3 Study 4311
Study 4311 was a multi-center, open-label, randomized-controlled study in which 52 adult and adolescent patients (27 HAwI, and 25 HBwI), previously treated with on-demand therapy, were randomized 1:2 to arm 1, no prophylaxis (on demand therapy) (N=19) or arm 2, concizumab prophylaxis (N=33) for at least 24 weeks. Arms 3 and 4 included 81 additional patients (53 HAwI and 28 HBwI) treated with concizumab prophylaxis. Any bleeds occurring in the clinical study were treated with the patient's usual bypassing
The benefit of concizumab was clearly demonstrated in Study 4311, in which prophylactic treatment with daily concizumab reduced spontaneous and traumatic bleeding episodes in patients with HAwI and HBwI. This was evident by the reduction in the number of bleeding events in patients treated with concizumab prophylaxis compared to on demand therapy. While there is a concern that patients may alter their behavior due to knowledge of their treatment assignment, the highly statistically significant efficacy result provides reassurance of treatment effect.
L289: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
product or factor. Superiority of concizumab PPX (arm 2) over no PPX (arm 1) was confirmed for the primary endpoint of number of treated spontaneous and traumatic bleeding episodes. The annualized bleeding rate (ABR) was calculated as the number of treated spontaneous or traumatic bleeds divided by the number of days in the analysis set, multiplied by 365. The ABR was 1.7 with 95% CI (1.01, 2.87) for patients on concizumab PPX (arm 2) and 11.8 with 95% CI (7.03, 19.86) for patients on no PPX (arm 1). The estimated ABR ratio was 0.14 with 95% CI (0.07, 0.29), corresponding to an 86% reduction in ABR for patients on concizumab PPX (arm 2) compared to no PPX (arm 1). Results were statistically significant and the magnitude of reduction in bleeding rate was clinically meaningful for a patient population at risk for frequent bleeding that is difficult to treat.
Key secondary endpoints of change in SF-36v2 bodily pain and change in SF-36v2 physical functioning were not statistically significant.
In a descriptive analysis, 94.7% of patients in arm 1 (no PPX) had a treated bleed or discontinued before week 24 compared to 48.5% in arm 2 (concizumab PPX). )
Supportive, Phase 2 Study 4310
Study 4310 was a supportive, multicenter, open-label, randomized-controlled study in which 26 patients (16 HAwI patients, 10 HBwI) patients were randomized 2:1 to concizumab PPX (n=17) and eptacog alfa (rFVIIa) on demand (N=9) for at least 24 weeks. The primary endpoint was the number of bleeding episodes during at least 24 weeks from treatment onset. The ABR was significantly lower in the concizumab PPX arm compared to the eptacog arm. The estimated ABR during the entire treatment period was also lower in the concizumab arm compared to the eptacog alfa arm: 5.4 (95% CI: 3.8, 7.6) and 20.6 (95% CI 13.7, 30.8), respectively.
Substantial evidence of effectiveness of concizumab for prophylaxis in patients with HAwI and HBwI was based on one adequate and well controlled study, Study 4311, and confirmatory evidence. Confirmatory evidence was provided from Study 4310 based on a large reduction in ABR in the concizumab arm compared to the eptacog alfa arm in patients with HAwI and HBwI, along with mechanistic support of reduction in TFPI with corresponding increased thrombin generation.
L290: Risk and risk management	
Overall Safety Findings
The review of safety focused on Study 4311, which included 127 patients with HAwI and HBwI exposed to concizumab, including 33 patients randomized to concizumab PPX (arm 2) compared to 19 patients randomized to no PPX (arm 1) in the main treatment period. The safety analysis was further supported by Study 4310.
TEAEs were reported in 60% of patients in concizumab PPX arm 2 compared to 42% patients in the on-demand arm 1 in the randomized main treatment portion of study 4311. The most common TEAE (>10%) in the main treatment period and occurred in a higher proportion of patients in the concizumab arm compared to no PPX was local administration reactions, which occurred in 18% of patients in the concizumab arm (arm 2) compared to 0% in the on-demand therapy arm (arm 1). The most common ARs (>10%) at any timepoint (main and extension parts) after exposure to concizumab were local administration reaction (23.7%), nasopharyngitis (14.9%), and arthralgia (11.4%). Additional notable safety findings included one patient (<1%) with an AR of serious hypersensitivity reaction, prothrombin fragment 1.2 increased (6.1%), and fibrin d-dimer increased (5.3%).
Thrombosis
In total, three patients experienced non-fatal events of thrombosis, including arterial and venous thrombosis, after receiving concizumab, compared to no patients in the control group. Notable characteristics were additional risk factors for thrombosis (i.e., smoking, obesity and HTN), receiving factor product or a bypassing agent within 24 hours of the thrombotic event and elevated concizumab levels (>4000ng/mL).
The Applicant had modified the concizumab dosing regimen during the clinical development to target a concizumab level between 200-4000ng/mL, to prevent bleeding and thrombosis. In addition, the Applicant
Several risks have been identified that should be included in the Warnings and Precautions section of the USPI if concizumab is approved, this includes risk of thrombosis, hypersensitivity reactions, and increased laboratory values of fibrin D-dimer and prothrombin fragment 1+2. Local injection site reaction was the most common AR.
Patients with prolonged subtherapeutic levels (<200ng/mL) of concizumab are at increased risk of bleeding, which can be serious and life-threatening. Compliance to the daily SC dosing regimen of concizumab is critical to prevent prolonged subtherapeutic levels. Therefore, the importance of adherence to daily dosing should be stressed in labeling and a Medication Guide. In addition, a plan for missed concizumab doses should be described in labeling.
Concizumab levels >200ng/mL is key to the therapeutic effect of concizumab and levels <4000ng/mL is critical to decrease the risk of thrombosis, therefore it is necessary to have an accurate and reliable concizumab ELISA in order to dose adjust concizumab to a safe and efficacious concentration. CDRH has identified multiple issues with the concizumab ELISA and has determined it is inadequate for postmarket use.
L291: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
included a number of mitigation strategies, including using the lowest labeled dose of factor or bypassing agent. After the new dosing regimen was put in place along additional mitigation strategies, no additional thrombotic events occurred in clinical studies.
Risk of Bleeding with Subtherapeutic Concizumab Levels.
The majority of patients with subtherapeutic concizumab levels (<200 ng/mL) had no bleeding or minor bleeding although, a serious bleeding event of muscle hemorrhage and a fatal intracranial bleed occurred with subtherapeutic levels.
Large PK fluctuations were noted in clinical studies, this was in part due to issues with non-compliance. PK analysis demonstrates missed doses of concizumab can result in subtherapeutic levels within a few days, further, once the patient restarts administration it may take several days to become therapeutic.
L292: Abbreviations: ABR, annualized bleeding rate; aPCC, activated prothrombin complex concentrates; ELISA, enzyme-linked immunoassay; HA, hemophilia A; HB, hemophilia B; HAwI, hemophilia A with inhibitors; HBwI, hemophilia B with inhibitors; HTN, hypertension; ITI, immune tolerance induction; PK, pharmacokinetic; PPX, prophylaxis; SAEs, serious adverse events; SC, subcutaneous; TEAEs, treatment-emergent adverse events; TFPI, tissue factor pathway inhibitor; USPI, United States Prescribing Information
L293: ## 2.2. Conclusions Regarding Benefit-Risk
L294: BLA 761315, ALHEMO® (concizumab), provided substantial evidence of effectiveness based on data from clinical studies. However, the application will receive a Complete Response letter because of the inspection, multiple product quality deficiencies, an inadequate interim assay to monitor concizumab concentration after dose adjustment and an inadequate plan for monitoring for missed doses that preclude approval.
L295: The review teams acknowledged that the hemophilias are serious and life-threatening diseases with few to no treatment options depending upon the subtype. If left untreated, hemophilia will result in significant morbidity and early mortality from uncontrolled bleeding arising spontaneously, following injury, or related to surgery. Thus, affected patients start on a life-long treatment program of blood products or recombinant factor concentrates designed to “bypass” the absent clotting factor, or emicizumab to prevent severe bleeding for patients with hemophilia A with and without inhibitors. Some patients treated with blood products and recombinant factor concentrates will develop antibody inhibitors which render the treatment ineffective, leaving these patients with few to no options depending upon the subtype.
L296: Concizumab focuses on providing a prophylaxis treatment for those with congenital hemophilia A or B with inhibitors. For the subgroup of patients with congenital hemophilia B with inhibitors this product has the potential to address an unmet medical need because of a lack of available therapies available for prophylaxis. The nonclinical, clinical, statistical and clinical pharmacology teams support the approval based on the nonclinical and clinical data packages and did not identify any deficiencies. No important differences of opinion were voiced during the review process.
L297: The clinical data package consisted of several clinical studies that demonstrated an effect on ABR, an endpoint that has been used for the approval of other monoclonal antibodies or blood products for the treatment of hemophilia. The main study was 4311, an international, multicenter, open-label, randomized controlled study. Study 4311 was designed to compare “on demand” treatment with a bypassing agent, eptacog alfa or activated prothrombin complex concentrate, administered at the time of bleeding with prophylaxis concizumab treatment. The efficacy results demonstrated a markedly lower reduction in median annualized bleeding rates for the prophylaxis concizumab treatment when compared with the “on demand” eptacog alfa or activated prothrombin complex concentrate treatment. Median ABR for the concizumab combined arms was 1.7 [1.01, 2.87] bleeds compared with 11.8 [7.03, 19.86] bleeds for the eptacog alfa treatment “on demand” arm. During the initial part of the study, an increase in thrombotic events occurred in the concizumab treatment arm which resulted in a study pause and clinical hold because of concerns of excessive TFPI inhibition. Thus, Novo Nordisk developed an algorithm based on concizumab pharmacokinetics (PK) levels to adjust concizumab dose if needed. The study resumed and no further thrombotic events were observed. The major Study 4311 concizumab safety findings were bleeding, thrombosis, hypersensitivity reactions, and injection site reactions.
L298: The clinical pharmacology review concurred with the Applicant’s assessment of 200 ng/mL as the lower end of the target concentration for concizumab. Levels lower than 200 ng/mL are associated with increased risk for bleeding. During the review, the team noted that for some
L299: patients there was significant intrasubject PK fluctuation, which the Applicant attributed to target mediated drug disposition (TMDD). The clinical pharmacology review team considered other scenarios potentially accounting for the observed results including antidrug antibodies, problems with the PK assay, and membrane bound TFPI and treatment compliance issues. The issue of missed dosing (compliance) is a significant concern because there would be a potential for bleeding risk for the few patients whose exposure falls below 200 ng/mL. Because spontaneous bleeding events and sites cannot be predicted, understanding how patients and their healthcare providers should handle missed doses is an important key to the safe and effective use of concizumab. The importance of adherence to daily dosing and maintenance of adequate levels should be stressed in any labeling, Medication Guide and educational material.
L300: The nonclinical review did not identify any unexpected risks with concizumab. The nonclinical toxicology studies' results were consistent with the expected mechanism of action of TFPI inhibition. Toxicities observed reflected the expected consequence of higher-than-expected exposures than those anticipated at the proposed therapeutic dose.
L301: Ovo Nordisk proposed an interim assay and process which would be available for a concizumab launch. CDRH's review identified deficiencies for the interim assay and plan which include assay accuracy, precision, sample handling, and methodology.
L302: Besides the issues with the interim assay, the Agency's product reviewers identified chemistry, manufacturing, and controls issues: the failed facilities inspection and multiple deficiencies including inadequate cell bank stability protocols, inadequate reference material stability protocols, inadequate DS and DP release and stability specifications, and inadequate DS and DP post-approval annual stability protocols.
L303: ## II. Interdisciplinary Assessment
L304: ## 3. Introduction
L305: ## Product Introduction
L306: Concizumab (proposed proprietary name, ALHEMO®), a new molecular entity (NME), is a monoclonal antibody antagonist of endogenous tissue factor pathway inhibitor (TFPI). It is directed against TFPI which allows increased downstream Fxa production by the coagulation factor VIIa (FVIIa)/tissue factor (TF) complex and further downstream generation of thrombin in the coagulation cascade. Concizumab acts independently from FVIII and coagulation factor IX (FIX).
L307: The Applicant proposes the following indication: for routine prophylaxis to prevent or reduce the frequency of bleeding episodes in adult and pediatric patients with hemophilia A with inhibitors (HAWI) and hemophilia B with inhibitors (HBwI).
L308: The proposed dosing regimen:
L309: - Day 1: Loading dose of 1 mg/kg
- Day 2: Once-daily dose of 0.20 mg/kg until individualization of maintenance dose (see below)
- 4 weeks after initiation of treatment: For dose optimization measure concizumab plasma concentration once by concizumab enzyme-linked immunoassay (ELISA) prior to administration of next scheduled dose
- After concizumab plasma concentration result is available but recommended no later than 8 weeks after initiation of treatment: Individualize maintenance dose of concizumab once based on the following concizumab plasma concentrations:&lt;200 ng/mL: adjust to a once-daily dose of 0.25 mg/kg
- 200 to 4000 ng/mL: continue once-daily dose of 0.20 mg/kg
- &gt;4000 ng/mL: adjust to a once-daily dose of 0.15 mg/kg
L310: ## Disease Background
L311: Patients with congenital hemophilia A or B lack specific clotting factors (factor VIII or IX, respectively) and thus have spontaneous bleeding events which can affect critical organs and be fatal. Patients with hemophilia A and B are typically treated with blood/recombinant factor concentrates which supply the necessary missing clotting factors. Some patients with hemophilia will develop antibodies (also known as inhibitors) to the factor concentrates rendering the factor concentrates ineffective to prevent bleeding events. These patients who have hemophilia and inhibitors are then treated with a bypassing strategy designed to overcome the missing factor and its inhibitor.
L312: ## HAwI
L313: Congenital hemophilia A (HA) is an X-linked bleeding disorder characterized by the deficiency or absence of factor VIII (FVIII). Hemophilia A occurs in 1 in 5000 live male births, without ethnic predominance, which results in an incidence in the US of about 20,000 people and 400,000 people worldwide (Soucie et al. 1998). The absence of functional FVIII interrupts the coagulation cascade and the ability to form a stable clot in response to trauma. The resulting bleeding complications can be severe and life threatening and can lead to long-term morbidity from joint damage due to recurrent bleeds. The standard of care in patients with HA is treatment with recombinant or plasma-derived FVIII concentrates. Treatment can either be in response to a bleeding event (episodic or “on demand” treatment) or scheduled treatment to prevent bleeds (prophylactic treatment). In resource-rich countries, prophylactic treatment initiated prior to any joint bleeds is preferred. In 30-35% of patients with hemophilia A, a neutralizing antibody, known as a FVIII inhibitor, forms which renders exogenous and endogenous FVIII nonfunctional. Patients with FVIII inhibitors are more difficult to treat in response to bleeding events, leading to a higher rate of progressive joint disease from frequent joint bleeds and an increased risk of death from uncontrolled bleeding.
L314: ## HBwI
L315: Congenital hemophilia B (HB) is an X-linked bleeding disorder caused by the deficiency of FIX, a vitamin K-dependent plasma protease that participates in the intrinsic blood coagulation pathway and whose function is to cleave and activate FX within the coagulation cascade. The prevalence of the disease is estimated to be 1:30,000 males (Santoro et al. 2018). Bleeding tendency in HB is in accordance with the severity of factor deficiency. Patients with the severe form (FIX &lt;1%), who account approximately for 30 to 45% of persons with HB, usually suffer from recurrent joint, soft-tissue, and muscle bleedings. FIX concentrates are administered in order to reduce bleeding complications (Santoro et al. 2018). The reported rate of FIX inhibitors is 1 to 15% of all patients with HB (Male et al. 2021).
L316: ## Current Therapy Options
L317: Current therapeutic options for patients with HAwI and HBwI are summarized below (Table 3).
L318: ## HAwI
L319: Patients with inhibitors cannot be treated with FVIII replacement as the inhibitor would clear the exogenous FVIII and likely increase the inhibitor titer. To clear the FVIII inhibitor, patients may undergo immune tolerance induction (ITI). ITI involves frequent administrations of high-dose FVIII to eliminate the antibody over time and allows patients to resume their prophylactic treatment with FVIII. While this is successful in some patients, there is a failure rate of 20-40%, and those patients who have a resurgence of their antibody after ITI tend to be even more refractory to treatment. ITI also presents a heavy burden to patients both in terms of effort and cost from daily high dose factor VIII infusions. The available treatments in patients with FVIII inhibitors are episodic treatment with a bypassing agent which bypasses FVIII in the coagulation cascade, often during ITI. Unfortunately, the efficacies of these agents are unstable compared to FVIII infusions for patients without inhibitors. Table 3 below summarizes the available therapies
L320: for the treatment of HAwI. NovoSeven® and FEIBA® are available agents have product-specific characteristics that limit their usability. NovoSeven is a recombinant activated factor VII (rFVIIa) product which has a short half-life and requires intravenous (IV) treatment every two to three hours. FEIBA, or Factor Eight Inhibitor Bypassing Activity, is a plasma-derived activated prothrombin complex concentrate (aPCC) which is predominately composed of prothrombin and Factor X, but also contains lesser amounts of Factors VII and IX. This product requires a long infusion time which can limit its use. It also contains small amounts of FVIII, resulting in the risk of augmenting inhibitor formation in patients not currently undergoing ITI. Patients with FVIII inhibitors can also receive prophylactic treatment with a bypassing agent. FEIBA was approved in 2013 for this indication. The primary efficacy outcome for the registration study was annualized bleeding rate (ABR), which is the rate of bleeds normalized to one year. Patients on FEIBA prophylaxis had a median ABR of 8 compared to 29 for patients receiving episodic treatment with FEIBA. Prophylactic treatment is costly and burdensome given the need for every-other-day IV infusions of FEIBA. NovoSeven is not approved for prophylaxis with the exception of prevention of bleeding during surgical or invasive procedures, and therefore the dosing regimens used are not standardized and with variable rates of efficacy.
L321: Emicizumab is a humanized monoclonal modified immunoglobulin G4 (IgG4) bispecific factor Ixa-and factor X-directed antibody, which was approved for marketing on November 16, 2017, under BLA 761083. Emicizumab is indicated for routine prophylaxis to prevent or reduce the frequency of bleeding episodes in adult and pediatric patients ages newborn and older with HA with or without factor VIII inhibitors (i.e., HA and HAwI). The recommended loading dose of emicizumab is 3 mg/kg administered by SC injection once weekly for the first 4 weeks, followed by a maintenance dose of 1.5 mg/kg once every week, or 3 mg/kg once every two weeks, or 6 mg/kg once every four weeks. The selection of a maintenance emicizumab dose is based on healthcare provider preference with consideration of regimens that may increase patient adherence. The emicizumab product label contains a boxed warning that states cases of thrombotic microangiopathy and thrombotic events were reported when on average a cumulative amount of &gt;100 U/kg/24 hours of aPCC was administered for 24 hours or more to patients receiving emicizumab prophylaxis. The boxed warning contains the recommendation to monitor for the development of thrombotic microangiopathy and thrombotic events if aPCC is administered, and to discontinue aPCC and suspend dosing of emicizumab if symptoms occur. The most common adverse reactions (incidence \ge10% of patients) reported in clinical studies are injection site reactions, headache, and arthralgia.
L322: ## HBwI
L323: Treatment of hemorrhagic episodes in patients with HBwI must be individualized. The choice of therapy depends on the type of bleeding episode, the inhibitor titer, and the history and severity of previous infusion reactions. Bypassing agents (activated prothrombin complex concentrate [aPCC]; activated recombinant FVII [rFVIIa]) are therapeutic options for treatment or prevention of bleeding episodes in high-responding inhibitor patients. Bypassing agents have been shown to be effective in acute bleeding control in ~80% of patients with HBwI (Santoro et al. 2018). The presence of FIX in aPCC, in patients with previous inhibitor recognition, generally requires administration of rFVIIa. In cases of life-threatening bleeding episodes and failure of treatment with bypassing agents, plasmapheresis or immunoadsorption with staphylococcal protein A may
L324: be considered to remove anti-FIX antibodies, and to transiently restore replacement therapy with FIX (Santoro et al. 2018). Because of the lack of therapeutic options for a large subpopulation of patients with HBwI, concizumab would address an unmet medical need.
