L723: ## **Assessment**
L724: The issue of significant PK fluctuation in few individual patients was communicated to the Applicant. The Applicant associates the high degree of inter- and intra-subject variability to concizumab's TMDD. While a high inter-subject PK variability of concizumab can be expected based on varying target pool between patients, the magnitude of within-subject PK changes, as noted in few individuals, is unlikely to be completely explained by TMDD alone. Therefore, the review team considered other factors that may play a role in these observations. These are discussed in detail below:
L725: - **ADAs:** A total of 16 out of the 42 patients with concizumab exposure levels below 200 ng/mL tested positive for binding ADAs at some point during maintenance dose setting and up until PACO. Most occurrences were transient, and titers were very low. Three patients had in vitro neutralizing antibodies at one or a few visits (Subjects [REDACTED](b) (6)); however, binding titers were very low, and no increased bleeding were observed in these patients. In general, there was no association between occurrence of ADAs and bleeding episodes (if any) for patients testing positive for ADAs. The immunogenicity analysis based on comparison between ADA positive and negative patients also supports a lack of impact on concizumab PK and PD due to ADAs (See Section 14.4).
- **PK assay:** ELISA that was used in the clinical study was adequately validated and accepted by the Agency as fit-for-purpose to determine concizumab plasma concentrations. During the study, PK and PD samples were collected in a time-matched manner. Considering that the concentration of free TFPI (PD marker) was determined using a commercial assay and which mostly shows an inverse relationship with PK, it suggests that the large fluctuation in concizumab concentration is unlikely to be due to any imprecision with the PK assay.
- **Membrane-bound TFPI:** Concizumab clearance is mainly mediated by TMDD, where concizumab binds to endothelial cell-anchored TFPI (the target) with subsequent endocytosis and degradation (target-mediated clearance). It is not clear if the expression of membrane bound TFPI could increase during concizumab treatment and lead to increased drug clearance. However, the mean C_{ave} of concizumab in all patients receiving concizumab was relatively stable over the treatment period, which suggests that increased target synthesis may be unlikely and cannot explain the large PK variability observed in a small portion of patients.
L726: Lastly, one plausible explanation for significant changes in drug levels during treatment could be due to lack of patient adherence to daily SC injection of concizumab. As noted earlier, based on concizumab's PK characteristics, administration once-daily is critical to avoid large peak-to-trough fluctuations. The assessment of the review team related to patient compliance is discussed below:
L727: - The impact of missed doses on concizumab concentrations was demonstrated in Study 4159. In this study, concizumab was dosed at 0.25, 0.5 and 0.8 mg/kg on Day 1, Day 2, and every 3 days thereafter. As all concizumab SC injections were administered at the study site, the dosing records were reliable and complete, which allows for the analysis on the impact of missing doses on concizumab PK. Table 31 shows the pre-dose concizumab concentrations at each visit in the 6 subjects who were assigned to the 0.8 mg/kg group (equivalent to
L728: 0.27 mg/kg/day). Although there is some degree of concentration fluctuation at each visit, a more than 10-fold change in PK concentration within individual patients is primarily associated with missing doses. For example, subject ID (b) (6) (represented by the blue line) had a big drop in concentration from 3140 ng/mL on Day 5 to 346 ng/mL on Day 11 due to one missed dose on Day 8. Similar trends for larger drops in concentration with a missed dose were also observed in other patients (highlighted in red).
L729: - For patient ID (b) (6), at the two study visits where drug dosing was done on site and when rich PK samples were collected, concizumab concentrations were 1280 ng/mL at the end of Day 1 and 2890 ng/mL at Week 24. However, at other visits where only pre-dose PK samples were collected, concizumab levels were mostly below the target level of 200 ng/mL (Table 30). The reason for such a pattern is hard to explain for anything other than patient noncompliance. To corroborate this inference, the patient's e-Diary shows multiple incidences of missing dose for more than four consecutive days (See Section 7.7.3). As per the Applicant, the trend for lower concentrations coincided with large number of missing doses in this patient. In addition, protocol violation for missing doses were also reported in other patients.
- The significant impact of missed dosing on concizumab concentration is also consistent with the PK characteristics of this drug. As a monoclonal antibody, the PK of concizumab is characterized by a combination of saturable non-linear TMDD and nonspecific linear elimination processes. At the target concentration range, TMDD plays a dominant role in the clearance of concizumab leading to a rapid clearance and a short half-life. As illustrated in Figure 12, following administration of a single SC dose of 1 mg/kg, concizumab concentrations drop steeply from a Cmax of around 2000 ng/mL to &lt;200 ng/mL within a couple of days without subsequent dosing. When concentrations drop to low subtherapeutic levels due to missed doses, in the absence of a loading dose, it may take several days of consecutive dosing to gradually return to target concentrations of &gt;200 ng/mL (light blue line in Figure 13).
L730: Taken together, these assessments strongly suggests that patient compliance to daily administration of concizumab is critical to maintain therapeutic concentrations.

L744: ## Conclusion
L745: Overall, based on the PK characteristics of concizumab and observation in Study 4159, patient compliance to daily administration is critical to maintain therapeutic concentrations. Therefore,
L746: the Applicant should propose labeling language that clearly informs of the risks of missed doses, the importance of adherence prior to the measurement of concizumab concentrations after four weeks of treatment, and a proposal for how to initiate the next dose after missed dose(s) (e.g., repeat loading dose?). The Applicant should also enhance patient education on adherence to therapy, likely by including a Medication Guide in FDA labeling.
L747: ## 7.3. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Identified Through Postmarket Experience
L748: Concizumab is not currently approved or marketed in the United States or elsewhere.
L749: ## 7.4. FDA Approach to the Safety Review
L750: The safety review focuses on the Phase 3 Study 4311. Supportive safety information was obtained from the Phase 2 dose escalation Study 4310. Studies were analyzed separately due to differences in study design and concizumab dosing regimen.
L751: The safety database of Study 4311 included a total of 133 adolescent or adult patients with HAwI or HBwI, of which 127 patients were exposed to concizumab PPX in either the main treatment period or the extension period. In total, there were 19 patients in the no PPX arm 1 in the main treatment portion (randomized portion consisting of 24 weeks of study drug therapy) of the study and 13 patients continued the study to receive concizumab prophylaxis (PPX) in the extension portion of the study. In arm 2 (concizumab PPX arm) of the study, 33 patients were evaluated in the main treatment portion of the study. In total, there were 81 patients in arms 3 to 4 who received concizumab PPX, which includes patients who were enrolled in the main treatment portion of the study, the extension portion, and follow-up parts of the study.
L752: Support for the safety database included 26 adult patients with HAwI or HBwI from Study 4310, of which 25 patients were exposed to concizumab in either main treatment period or the extension period.
L753: The data cutoff date for the safety assessments submitted in the BLA was March 10, 2022. The 120-Day safety update provided additional safety data collected from ongoing clinical studies, including Study 4311 through July 11, 2022.
L754: Review of safety was based upon:
L755: - Clinical Study Report (CSR) for studies
- Protocol for studies
- Data sets for the populations described above
- Summary of clinical safety
- 120-day safety update
- Patient narratives
- Case report forms
L756: Case report forms and narratives were provided and reviewed for adverse events (AEs) of interest, serious adverse events (SAEs), and deaths. Treatment-emergent adverse events (TEAEs) of interest were thromboembolic events.
L757: No major issues were identified with the respect to recoding, coding, and categorizing AEs. The analysis dataset for adverse events data file was reviewed for accuracy of translation of the verbatim to preferred term by manual review.
L758: ## 7.5. Adequacy of the Clinical Safety Database
L759: The Applicant reported that a total of 185 unique patients with HAwI and HBwI and HA have been exposed to concizumab, corresponding to a total of 228.6 patient-years exposed (PYE) in the overall concizumab clinical development program. Across the concizumab clinical development program, 148 (80%) patients of the pooled population were exposed to concizumab for at least 6 months, corresponding to the duration of the main part in Study 4311 and Study 4310.
L760: In Study 4311, the median duration of the main treatment period was 31.1 weeks (range 3.9, 72.9 weeks) in arm 1 (No PPX) compared to a longer duration of therapy of 45.0 weeks (range 10.0, 58.3 weeks) in arm 2 (concizumab PPX). The proportion of patients who completed between 32 and 52 weeks of therapy was 6/19 (32%) patients in arm 1 and 22/33 (66.7%) patients in arm 2. Median duration based on the main treatment and extension period for arms 2 to 4 was 40.7 weeks (range 32.1, 68.2 weeks).
L761: Overall, the duration of therapy was adequate to evaluate the safety of concizumab compared to no PPX. The reviewer table below summarizes the duration of exposure in the safety population in Study 4311.
L762: In Study 4310 only patients on previous on-demand therapy were enrolled. The median duration of treatment in study 4310 was 39.3 weeks (range 23.3, 48.1 weeks) in the concizumab arm and 23.9 weeks (range 10, 24.7 weeks) in the eptacog alfa arm before the data cut-off for the main part of the study. The median duration of therapy in the 24 week main treatment period and extension portions of the study was 30.3 weeks (21.3, 46.1 weeks) in the concizumab arm and 2.4 weeks (range 1.0, 5.0 weeks) in the eptacog alfa arm in the main portion of the study. After 24 weeks patients in the eptacog alfa arm crossed over to concizumab treatment. There were 16 out of 17 patients in the concizumab arm in Study 4310 who were treated with concizumab for at least 24 weeks. No patients randomized to the eptacog alfa arm received eptacog alfa beyond 24 weeks.

L773: ## 7.6. Safety Results
L774: ## 7.6.1. Safety Results, Study 4311
L775: ## 7.6.1.1. Overview of Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events
## Summary, Study 4311
L776: In Study 4311, more patients in the concizumab PPX arm 2 experienced TEAEs compared to the no PPX arm 1, 60.6% versus 42.1%, respectively. AEs in either treatment arm were primarily reported as mild severity. A similar proportion of patients in arms 1 and 2 reported SAEs in the main part of the study, although more patients reported adverse events (AEs) leading to study drug dose modification in arm 2 compared to arm 1, 15.2% versus 0%, respectively. AEs leading to treatment dose modification in arm 2 were headache, dizziness, melena, each reported in one patient and COVID-19 infection reported two patients. The reviewer's table below shows an overview of adverse events in Study 4311.

L802: **Clinical Reviewer Comment:** *The case of death related to COVID-19 does not appear to be related to concizumab therapy. One patient died due to multiple fractures from a motor vehicle accident (MVA). While the details are limited in this case narrative, it is possible the patient died to bleeding related complications. As a recent concizumab level was not reported, it is not clear if the patient had a subtherapeutic level at the time of the event, increasing the risk of fatal bleeding.*
L803: ## **Fatal Cases Reported in the 120-Safety Update Period**
L804: - Patient ID (b) (6). This patient was a male age 44 years, with HBwI. The patient was treated in arm 2 of the study (concizumab PPX) for 375 days. The patient had an alcohol related
L805: coma with respiratory arrest. Three days later the patient died due to hypoxic cardiorespiratory arrest.
L806: - Patient ID (b)(6). This patient was a male age 13 years with HBwI. In the 24 weeks prior to study enrollment, 3 bleeding episodes and 5 spontaneous bleeding episodes (&lt;9 bleeding episodes) were reported. The patient was randomized to treatment arm 2. Concizumab doses were administered at the site at visit 2a and 9a. The patient was treated in arm 2 with concizumab. He had his individual maintenance dose set at visit 6a, in which the dose of concizumab was increased to 0.25 mg/kg due to a subtherapeutic level. All other injections were to be administered at home by the patient or caregiver. On study day 345, the patient developed an intense headache and repeated vomiting and was instructed by the investigator to go to the nearest emergency room (ER). The following day, the mother took the patient to the ER. A CT scan was performed and showed an intracranial hemorrhage that required surgical intervention with hematoma drainage. Surgery was performed the same day. Six days following the surgery, the patient died after presenting tachycardia with hypotension and subsequently cardio-respiratory arrest (death diagnosis: brain death). According to the Applicant, the patient's mother confirmed that the bleeding event was spontaneous and there was no recent history of trauma. There was no family or personal history of intracerebral hemorrhage or aneurysms.
L807: The Applicant reported that the patient had no bleeding events prior to the fatal bleeding event while on study. The Applicant reported that, "missing doses for more than 4 consecutive days were identified for this subject in the following periods: 30 Sep 2021–5 Oct 2021 (6 missing doses); 28 Oct 2021–26 Dec 2022 (59 missing doses); 4 Jan 2022–26 Jan 2022 (6 doses); 16 Feb 2022–13 Mar 2022 (28 missing doses); 2 Mar 2022–07 Apr 2022 (missing 15 doses)." In addition, "According to the medical note, it was confirmed that doses were taken but not documented in the eDiary by the study subject." The Applicant reported that the patient's concizumab levels during the last 6 months prior to the fatal event (onset date (b)(6)) peaked at 1130 ng/mL in end (b)(6) (visit 9a) and declined to 223 ng/mL end (b)(6) (visit 10a), to 212 ng/mL (b)(6) (visit 11a) and to 28.1 ng/mL mid (b)(6) (visit 12a). There was inverse relationship between concizumab exposure and free TFPI levels over time according to the Applicant after concizumab was administered on site. No neutralizing antibody was reported.
L808: *Clinical Reviewer Comment: The fatal case of patient (b)(6) is concerning because it appears the patient was likely non-compliant with concizumab PPX, which may have caused, or at least contributed, to the fatal event. This clinical reviewer recommends that the Applicant submit a Medication Guide to address the need for good patient education about compliance with treatment.*

L837: Median analysis duration based on the data are 31.1 weeks (No PPX Arm 1 Main), 45 weeks (Concizumab PPX Arm 2 Main), 49.4 weeks (arm 1 Extension), 55.3 weeks (Arm 2 Overall), 70.4 weeks (Arm 3 Overall), and 33.1 weeks (arm 4 Overall).
L838: Overall includes the main, extension, and follow-up parts of the study. Arm 1 Extension includes the extension and follow-up parts of the study.
L839: Risk difference (with 95% confidence interval) is shown between treatment and comparator. An asterisk (*) indicates the 95% confidence interval excludes zero.
L840: Each FMQ is aligned to a single SOC based on clinical judgment. However, please be aware that some FMQs may contain PTs from more than one SOC.
L841: Some preferred terms are not included in any FDA medical query. Those preferred terms are not shown or counted in this table.
L842: For specific preferred terms under each FMQ, see the table "Serious Adverse Events by System Organ Class, FDA Medical Query (Narrow) and Preferred Term..."
L843: Arm 1 and arm 2 consist of patients previously treated on demand. Arm 3 consists of patients transferred from Phase 2 Study 4310. Arm 4 consists of patients previously treated on PPX or on demand.
L844: Abbreviations: AE, adverse event; CI, confidence interval; FMQ, FDA medical query; N, number of patients in treatment arm; n, number of patients with adverse event; PPX, prophylaxis; SOC, system organ class
L845: *Clinical Reviewer Comment: SAEs occurred infrequently in Study 4311, the most common serious adverse reaction in the entire study was viral infection, which occurred in 2 patients. Hemorrhage was not considered related to concizumab, as it is likely due to the underlying disease. Thrombotic events are discussed in Section 7.7.1. Of note, one patient in arm 2 of the clinical study experienced a serious hypersensitivity reaction 12 hours after exposure to concizumab. Symptoms included generalized erythema and pruritus with skin rash, persistent dry cough, and abdominal pain, leading to hospitalization. Concizumab was temporarily discontinued and symptoms improved with antihistamines and steroids. As this event was likely related to study drug, if approved, the product label should include a warning for hypersensitivity reactions.*
L846: ## 7.6.1.4. Adverse Events and FDA Medical Queries Leading to Treatment Discontinuation, Study 4311
L847: Adverse events (AEs) leading to treatment discontinuation were uncommon in Study 4311. In arm 2 there was 1 patient who had an adverse event (AE) leading to treatment discontinuation (i.e., renal infarct (reported as arterial thrombosis by narrow FDA medical query) during the randomized treatment portion of the study as shown in the table below.
L848: In total, 4 patients (3.5%) discontinued concizumab due to a TEAE during the entire study (main and extension portion).

L860: *Clinical Reviewer Comment: Drug discontinuation rates were low, supporting the tolerability of concizumab. Thrombotic events, including the case of renal infarct are discussed in Section 7.7.1.*
L861: *One patient discontinued due to a TEAE of congestive cardiomyopathy on study Day 4. Of note, the patient had pre-existing congestive cardiomyopathy, which worsened during the study. Due to the AE, the patient prematurely discontinued treatment on study Day 24 and later recovered. This case is confounded by pre-existing heart failure; therefore, attribution is difficult.*
L862: *One patient discontinued due to a hypersensitivity reaction. On study Day 14 (1 day after SC administration of concizumab in the abdomen), the patient had a non-serious event of injection site rash with burning and itching. Concizumab was temporarily discontinued for 7 days. After restarting concizumab the patient experienced a headache and fatigue which was treated with antihistamines. Following this, concizumab was permanently discontinued. Given the event was due to concizumab, along with another patient experiencing a serious hypersensitivity reaction following concizumab treatment (see Section 7.6.1.3 for further details), if approved, hypersensitivity reactions should be included as a warning in the product label.*
L863: ## 7.6.1.5. Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events, Study 4311
L864: TEAEs were reported in 60% of patients in arm 2 compared to 42% patients in arm 1 in the randomized treatment portion of Study 4311. With the exception of 2 patients in arm 2 reporting arthralgia, no AE was reported in \ge 2 patients in either treatment arm in the randomized treatment portion of Study 4311 as shown in the table below.
L865: Overall, in arms 2-4, 75 patients (65.8%) experienced a TEAE after exposure to concizumab. The most common TEAEs which occurred at any timepoint in the study (main and extension parts) were arthralgia (11.4%), injection site erythema (7.9%), upper respiratory tract infection (7%), prothrombin fragment 1.2 increased (6.1%), fibrin d-dimer increased (5.3%), headache (5.3%), COVID-19 (5.3%), nasopharyngitis (5.3%), and pyrexia (5.3%).

L875: The On-treatment observation period was used for assessment of AEs and is defined as the time period where subjects are considered to be affected by on demand treatment or concizumab treatment. L900: *Clinical Reviewer Comment: The most common TEAEs (&gt;10%) in the main treatment period and occurred in a higher proportion of patients in the concizumab arm compared to no PPX was local administration reactions. The preferred terms encompassed under this FMQ were injection site bruising, injection site erythema, injection site hemorrhage, injection site hematoma, injection site reaction, injection site urticaria, injection site induration, injection site pain, injection site pruritis, injection site rash, injection site swelling, and injection site hypoesthesia.*
L901: *The most common TEAEs (&gt;10%) at any timepoint (main and extension parts) after exposure to concizumab were local administration reaction (23.7%), nasopharyngitis (14.9%), hemorrhage (13.2%), and arthralgia (11.4%). Hemorrhage is not considered an adverse reaction as it is most likely related to the underlying disease.*
L902: *In total, 6% of patients had elevated prothrombin fragment and 5.3% had an elevated d-dimer in arms 2-4. No patients in the arm 1 (no concizumab PPX) had elevated prothrombin fragment or d-dimer. Elevated prothrombin fragment and d-dimer are associated with an increased risk of thrombosis therefore, if approved, the product label should include a warning for elevated prothrombin fragment and elevated d-dimer.*
L903: ## 7.6.1.6. Laboratory Findings, Study 4311
L904: There were no significant clinical laboratory changes in serum albumin (Alb), creatinine (Cr), alkaline phosphatase (AlkP), total bilirubin (Tbili), estimated glomerular filtration rate (eGFR, Cockroft-Gault), reported in Study 4311. There were three patients reported with elevation in serum AST, ALT (&gt;3x, &gt;5x and &gt;10x ULN, respectively). However, no patients reported elevations in serum Tbili in the randomized portion of the study. In the extension portion of the study there were 3 patients who had reported &gt;3x ULN serum Tbili, 1 patient with &gt;5x ULN and 1 patient with &gt;10x ULN. However, there were no patients who reported corresponding significant changes in serum AST or ALT.

L947: *Reviewer Comment: There were three patients reported with elevation in serum AST and ALT (&gt;3x, &gt;5x and &gt;10x ULN, respectively) in the extension portion of the study. Of note, none of the patients had concurrent elevation in bilirubin at the time of transaminitis. One of the three patients reported with elevated hepatic enzyme elevations had ALT&gt;5 UNL (519 U/L) and AST &gt;5 UNL (612 U/L) at Week 32 of concizumab therapy. The event was reported 1 day after the patient had an AE of COVID-19 (the AE of COVID-19 was fatal), therefore it is unlikely the elevated liver enzymes were related to concizumab. The remaining 2 cases appear to be potentially related to concizumab therapy as no other etiology for liver enzyme elevation was identified (no patient narratives provided).*
L948: *In addition, in the extension portion of the study there were 3 patients who had reported &gt;3x ULN serum Tbili, 1 patient with &gt;5x ULN and 1 patient with &gt;10x ULN. An etiology for hyperbilirubinemia was not identified (no patient narratives were provided). There were no patients who reported corresponding significant changes in serum AST or ALT.*
L949: *The elevated liver enzymes and hyperbilirubinemia is concerning, although with the exception of the patient with the fatal event due to COVID-19, none of the events of liver enzyme increase of bilirubin increase led to concizumab interruption or discontinuation. Further investigation is required to assess the hepatotoxicity risk, including a request for patient narratives which describes any concurrent illness and medications.*
L950: *There were two patients in Study 4311 in arm 1 (ID# [REDACTED](b)(6)) who did not have clinical laboratory postbaseline laboratory data during the randomized portion of the study. These patients were discontinued from the study for the following reasons.*
L951: - [REDACTED](b)(6): withdrawal by subject. This patient withdrew consent after discussing temporary closure of study due to the reported SAE of thromboembolism.
- [REDACTED](b)(6): death. This case is summarized in Section 7.6.1.2 Deaths, Study 4311.
L952: ## 7.6.1.7. Assessment of Drug-Induced Liver Injury, Study 4311
L953: The figure below shows a screening assessment for potential cases of serious drug-induced liver injury (DILI). No potential cases met Hy's Law criteria. One patient (as shown in Figure 14) had evidence of cholestasis (maximum Tbili (3.95xULN), without elevated liver enzymes. This patient had an elevated bilirubin at baseline.

L960: ## 7.6.1.8. Vital Signs' Analyses, Study 4311
L961: No clinically significant risk differences in vital signs were reported among patients in arm 1 compared to arm 2 in Study 4311. In the randomized portion of the study after 24 weeks the median systolic/diastolic blood pressure was 119/77 mm Hg (range 102-191/62-118 mm Hg) in arm 1 and 122/80 mm Hg (range 101-150/53-103 mm Hg) in Arm 2 in the randomized portion of the study. The median systolic/diastolic change from baseline was 3/1 mm Hg (range -16-23/-9-22 mm Hg) in arm 1 and 2/1 mm Hg (range -26-33/-17-32 mm Hg) in arm 2. Overall, the median heart rate was similar in both treatment arms after 24 weeks of therapy during the randomized portion of the study, i.e., 73-76 beats/min (range 55-129 beats/min). Overall, the median change in heart rate from baseline to Week 24 was -1 beats/min (range -29-50 beats/min) in both treatment arms.
L962: ## 7.6.1.9. Subgroup Analyses, Study 4311
L963: Overall, no significant differences in serious adverse events were reported between patients in treatment arms 1 and 2 based on race. However, most patients (67 of 118 (57%) enrolled in Study 4311 were white. Only 8 of 118 (7%) of patients enrolled in Study 4311 were black. There were 3 of 118 (3%) patients enrolled in Study 4311 were American Indian or Alaska Native. There were 5 of 118 (4%) of patients who did not have a race reported in Study 4311.
L964: *Clinical Reviewer Comment: No conclusions should be made based on subgroup analysis based on limited sample size.*
L965: ## 7.6.2. Safety Results, Study 4310
L966: ## 7.6.2.1. Overview of Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events
## Summary, Study 4310
L967: In Study 4310, there were 17 patients who were treated with concizumab PPX and 9 patients who were randomized to the no PPX arm. In total, there was 1 of 17 (6%) patients and 3 of 9 (33%) patients in the concizumab PPX and no PPX arms, respectively, who had a SAE reported. Overall, 13 of 17 (76%) patients in the concizumab PPX arm and 8 of 9 (89%) patients in the No PPX arm who had any AE reported. Most AEs were mild.

L979: ## 7.6.2.3. Serious Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events, Study 4310
L980: One SAE was reported among patients in the concizumab PPX arm, i.e., requirement for removal of a central venous catheter. SAEs reported in the no PPX arm included anemia, congestive cardiomyopathy and hordeolum in the randomized portion of the study, as shown in the table below.
L981: In patients exposed to concizumab at any time (randomized and extension periods), 20% of patients experienced an SAE. L991: Abbreviations: HAWI, hemophilia A with inhibitors; HBWI, hemophilia B with inhibitors; ISS, integrated summary of safety; N, number of patients in treatment arm; n, number of patients with adverse event; PPX, prophylaxis; SOC, system organ class
L992: Patient (b) (6) was a 34-year-old male with HBWI and baseline ABR of 45. He was receiving concizumab at 0.15 mg/kg. The patient had an accident at Week 32 resulting in bone fractures and required surgery. Concizumab was stopped and the patient developed hemorrhagic shock a week later after a femoral artery puncture. Concizumab was restarted at Week 40. The patient developed a medium ADA titer with neutralizing antibodies, and the TFPI level returned to baseline. The ADA titer further increased to very high levels starting at Week 48. Per the Applicant, the patient's "bleeding pattern" did not change. The patient also had worsening liver function which was a pre-existing condition due to alcoholic fatty liver disease. At Week 72 the patient fell and developed a muscle and subdural hemorrhage. The patient was hospitalized and treatment was discontinued due to lack of therapeutic effect due to neutralizing antibody.
L993: *Clinical Reviewer Comment: One SAE occurred during the randomized treatment period, which was central line removal. This was an elective procedure and not related to concizumab treatment.*
L994: *One patient had multiple SAEs related to hemorrhage. While each AE was due to a traumatic event, the lack of therapeutic effect of concizumab due to neutralizing antibodies likely contributed. If concizumab is approved, the risk of neutralizing antibodies should be noted in the product label. See Section 14.4 for further details regarding immunogenicity.*
L995: ## 7.6.2.4. Adverse Events and FDA Medical Queries Leading to Treatment Discontinuation, Study 4310
L996: No adverse events (AEs) leading to treatment discontinuation were reported in Study 4310.
L997: ## 7.6.2.5. Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events, Study 4310
L998: The treatment-emergent adverse events reported in Study 4310 are shown in the table below. In the main treatment period, the most common TEAEs in the concizumab PPX arm were injection site hematoma (17.6%), nasopharyngitis (17.6%), headache (11.8%) and prothrombin level increased (11.8%). The most common TEAEs in the no PPX arm was contusion (22.2%).
L999: The most common TEAEs (&gt;10%) in patients exposed to concizumab in the main and extension periods of the study included diarrhea (16%), upper respiratory tract infection (16%), arthralgia (12%), and injection site hematoma (12%).

L1025: ## 7.6.2.6. Laboratory Findings, Study 4310
L1026: No clinically relevant differences in clinical laboratory testing results (i.e., serum albumin, serum creatinine, eGFR, alkaline phosphatase, ALT, AST, and total bilirubin, respectively, were observed between treatment arms in Study 4310, as shown in the tables below. A higher proportion of patients reported lymphopenia (&lt;1000 cells/\muL) in the concizumab PPX arm (3 of 17 (18% patients)) compared to the No PPX arm (0 of 9 (0% patients)) in Study 4310, as shown below. Overall, there were 6 patients with mild (&gt;4000 cells/\muL) increase. The mild changes lymphocyte counts or other minor blood cell count changes during concizumab therapy are of unclear clinical significance.

L1065: ## 7.7. Key Safety Review Issues
L1066: ## 7.7.1. Risk of Thrombosis
L1067: ## Issue Thrombosis
L1068: Three patients experienced non-fatal thrombotic events while receiving concizumab in clinical studies. The clinical reviewer assessed the safety data surrounding these events.
L1069: ## Background
L1070: Concizumab acts by enhancing the initiation phase of coagulation through increased activated coagulation factor X (Fxa) production, allowing sufficient thrombin generation and thus formation of clots. As a pro-coagulant, there is a risk of thrombosis with exaggerated pharmacology. Thrombosis formation was observed in non-clinical studies.
L1071: In clinical trials, three patients experienced thrombotic TEAEs while receiving concizumab. The clinical development program was subsequently put on a full clinical hold due to the potential for increased risk of thrombosis among patients treated with concizumab. The full clinical hold was removed after risk mitigation strategies were put in place as described below.
L1072: - Included a new guidance for treatment of breakthrough bleeds, with specific guidance for use of the lowest labeled dose of factor product or bypassing agent while on concizumab prophylaxis (PPX).
- Patients must contact the site when they have a suspected bleed.
- Continuation of previous PPX for up to two weeks after initiation of concizumab dosing was no longer allowed (Study 4307).
- A new concizumab dosing regimen including an initial daily dose of 0.20 mg/kg concizumab (instead of 0.25 mg/kg) was put in place. Criteria for an increase or decrease in the daily maintenance dose to 0.25 mg/kg or 0.15 mg/kg, respectively (based on concizumab exposure levels at the Week 4 visit 200 ng/mL-4000 ng/mL) were provided. The loading dose remained 1.0 mg/kg.
- Elective major surgery was no longer allowed in clinical studies.
- Trial stopping rule requiring urgent evaluation by the Novo Nordisk Safety Committee and consultation with the data monitoring committee (DMC) in case of one (instead of two)
L1073: significant thromboembolic event, disseminated intravascular coagulation (DIC), thrombotic microangiopathy (TMA) or death of study patient which may be related to the study product put in place.
L1074: ## Assessment
L1075: A summary of the three patients with thrombotic events while receiving concizumab is described in the table below. It is notable that both arterial and venous thrombotic events were reported 21 to 86 days after starting concizumab. In addition, all patients had additional risk factors for thrombosis including smoking, history of hypertension (HTN) and/or obesity. Two of the three patients had elevated concizumab levels &gt;4000ng/mL. Concizumab levels for each patient is described in the figure below. Lastly, all patients had received factor or a bypassing agent at the time of the thrombotic event. No patient had ADAs at the time of the event.
L1076: **Table 51. Summary of Thrombotic Events in Clinical Trials**

L1081: Figure 15. Concizumab Concentration Levels in Patients With Thrombotic Events
L1082: Source: Applicant's Document Investigations During Treatment Pause
L1083: Abbreviation: TE, thrombotic event; V, visit
L1084: One additional patient (Patient (b) (6)) age 42 years male with HAwI was treated with concizumab PPX at 0.25 mg/kg in the main part of the study prior to the treatment pause. Due to the treatment pause, the patient discontinued treatment with concizumab PPX on study Day 92. The patient did not re-initiate treatment with concizumab PPX after study restart and withdrew from the study on study Day 364. On study Day 106 (15 days after discontinuation of concizumab PPX), the patient had a non-serious event of infection in his left forearm following a shunt puncture. An ultrasound showed partial shunt stenosis. The patient had started treatment with eptacog alfa (activated) on study Day 104 (2 days prior to the event of shunt thrombosis) and initiated treatment with emicizumab (240 mg SC once weekly) on study Day 106 after which a complete shunt stenosis was reported. The thrombotic event for patient (b) (6) is not clearly related to concizumab therapy due to infection and treatment with additional coagulant factor and emicizumab.
L1085: After the clinical hold was removed and clinical studies were restarted, there were no thrombotic events.
L1086: ## Conclusion
L1087: Patients exposed to supratherapeutic doses of concizumab are at increased risk of thrombosis likely due to exaggerated pharmacologic effect. While the sample size is small, this risk appears to be increased in the setting of additional thrombotic risk factors, receiving concomitant factor
L1088: or bypassing agent, and concizumab levels &gt;4000\text{ng/mL}. The risk for thrombosis among patients undergoing concizumab therapy appears to have decreased once the Applicant included PK monitoring in the clinical development program, providing specific guidance for use of the lowest labeled dose of factor product or bypassing agent while on concizumab prophylaxis (PPX), and a new concizumab dosing regimen which targets concizumab levels &lt;4000\text{ng/mL}. If the product were to be approved, thromboembolic events should be included in the Warnings and Precautions section of the USPI. The new dosing regimen used after the treatment pause should be included in the USPI along with a statement to use the lowest effective dose of factor or bypassing agent to decrease the risk of thrombotic events. The frequency of PK monitoring is still unresolved at the time of this review and will be deferred to the next review cycle.
L1089: ## 7.7.2. Pharmacokinetic Fluctuations Leading to Subtherapeutic Concentrations During Concizumab Treatment
L1090: ## Issue: PK Fluctuations Leading to Subtherapeutic Concentrations During Concizumab Treatment
L1091: Due to PK fluctuations, there were patients with concizumab levels &lt;200\text{ng/mL}. Patients with concizumab levels &lt;200\text{ng/mL} are at increased risk of bleeding.
L1092: ## Background
L1093: Bleeding is inextricably linked with hemophilia and is commonly observed among the subset of patients with inhibitors. A concern for concizumab is the potential increase for bleeding if patients do not maintain the recommended concizumab plasma concentration of 200\text{ng/mL}-4000\text{ng/mL}, in particular a level over 200\text{ng/mL} (see Section 6.1 for further details). Significant fluctuations in plasma PK profiles were observed in individual patients. The review team (clinical pharmacology and clinical) investigated why PK fluctuations occurred and the potential impact on bleeding.
L1094: ## Assessment
L1095: The review team noted that 42 out of 97 patients (43%) had concizumab exposure levels below 200\text{ng/mL} at some point during the maintenance dose setting and up until primary analysis cut-off (PACO). In total, 7 out of 42 patients (16.7%) with subtherapeutic levels of concizumab (&lt;200\text{ng/mL}) had 11 AEs of bleeding in Study 4311. Nine out of 11 bleeding adverse events appear to be related to injection site reactions and were mild in severity. The remaining 2 events, occurring simultaneously in 1 patient, were severe and serious events related to muscle hemorrhage. No other bleeding episodes were reported for this patient. An additional patient reported in the 120-day safety update had a fatal bleeding event with a subtherapeutic level (28.1\text{ng/mL}) of concizumab. This patient is discussed further in Section 7.7.3.
L1096: A total of 16 patients out of the 42 patients with concizumab exposure levels below 200\text{ng/mL} at some point in time from maintenance dose to the end of data closure tested positive for binding ADAs. Most occurrences were transient, and titers were very low. Three patients had in
L1097: vitro neutralizing antibodies at 1 or a few visits (Subjects (b) (6)); however, binding titers were very low, and no clinical impact of ADAs was observed in these patients. Based on further analysis (see Section 14.4) immunogenicity does not appear to have impacted PK.
L1098: The Applicant believes the significant PK variability observed in some patients was due to TMDD. The review team believes this certainly contributed to PK fluctuations but could not solely explain the large PK fluctuations. As described in detail in Section 7.2, the most plausible explanation is the issue of compliance. Study 4159 demonstrated subtherapeutic concizumab levels within a few days of missed doses. In addition, when concentrations drop to low subtherapeutic levels due to missed doses, in the absence of a loading dose, it may take several days of consecutive dosing to gradually return to target concentrations of &gt;200 ng/mL.
L1099: ## Conclusion
L1100: While most bleeds that occurred when patients had subtherapeutic concizumab levels were minor, the potential remains for a moderate or severe or fatal bleeding events as observed in clinical studies. Outside of a controlled clinical study, risk of poor compliance to concizumab PPX will be greater and could pose a serious risk for the patient by increasing the potential for serious and fatal bleeding events. Given that a concizumab concentration above 200ng/mL is key to the therapeutic effect, the Applicant needs to provide a plan for a missed dose or multiple missed doses in labeling. This may include a plan for additional concizumab laboratory monitoring and/or a loading dose. In addition, given patients can become subtherapeutic quickly with missed doses, the Applicant should include a Medication Guide and address the need for good patient education and compliance with treatment.
L1101: ## 7.7.3. Patient Fatality Due to Bleeding and Subtherapeutic Concizumab Levels
L1102: ## Issue: Fatal Bleeding Event With Subtherapeutic Levels
L1103: A 13-year-old male with HBwI had a fatal intracranial hemorrhage while receiving concizumab PPX.
L1104: ## Background
L1105: Patient (b) (6) is summarized in Section 7.6.1.2 Deaths, Study 4311 in this review. Briefly, this patient was a male age 13 years with HBwI who developed a fatal intracranial bleed after 345 days of concizumab treatment. The patient had subtherapeutic concizumab levels at the time of the fatal event.
L1106: ## Assessment
L1107: The etiology of the fatal intracranial hemorrhage was investigated by the clinical reviewer. Based on the eDiary, the patient had multiple periods of 4 or more missed doses and missed doses corresponded to the subtherapeutic concizumab levels at visit 9a and 11a, see Table 30 during treatment.
L1108: The patient did not have ADAs near the time of the fatal event, so it is not likely that this contributed. During visits in which the patient received concizumab at the study site enhanced PK monitoring was conducted. The patient was therapeutic at those visits and there was inverse relationship between concizumab exposure and free TFPI levels over time.
L1109: Given the information gathered by the FDA reviewer the most likely explanation for the subtherapeutic concizumab level at the time of the fatal intracranial hemorrhage was non-compliance, as the patient did have therapeutic levels when administered concizumab on site and eDiary had documentation of missing doses which resulted in a protocol violation. No other patients had fatal bleeding events while utilizing concizumab.
L1110: ## Conclusion
L1111: In summary, patient (b) (6) was likely non-compliant with concizumab, and therefore was subtherapeutic and vulnerable to fatal bleeding. This case highlights the need for strict compliance, which can be difficult with a daily medication. Please refer to Section 7.7.2 regarding recommendations to improve patient compliance.
L1112: ## 8. Therapeutic Individualization
L1113: ## 8.1. Intrinsic Factors
L1114: The PK of concizumab is not influenced by age (12 to 79 years), race (White 62.9%, Asian 25.0%, Black 6.0%), hemophilia type (A versus B). Concizumab exposures are generally higher in healthy subjects compared to patients with hemophilia. The apparent clearance and volume of distribution of concizumab increased with increasing body weight (27 kg to 130.7 kg). See figure below for concizumab trough plasma concentration as a function of body weight following administration of concizumab 0.2 mg/kg. Nonetheless, after 4 weeks of treatment, patients with low or high exposure should adjust dose to 0.25 or 0.15 mg/kg, respectively, resulting in more similar exposure across patients.
L1115: No dedicated studies have been conducted to evaluate the impact of renal or hepatic impairment. As concizumab is a monoclonal antibody, there is no expectation for concizumab exposures to be different in patients with renal or hepatic impairment.
L1116: **Figure 16. Forest Plot of Covariate Effects on Concizumab Exposure (0.20 mg/kg)**
L1117: Source: Modelling Report on Population PK and exposure-response meta-analysis Phase 1, 2 and 3 data Figure 1-1
Data are expressed as steady-state average concizumab plasma concentrations (C_{avg}) following administration of 0.20 mg/kg relative to the reference subject profile, based on the full population PK model. The reference subject profile was non-Hispanic or Latino, White, adult male (\ge18 years), with a body weight of 70 kg not enrolled in Trial 4311. The points and bars (the column on the right) show point estimates (based on the maximum likelihood estimates) and 90% confidence intervals based on SIR relative to the reference subject. The reference body weight of 70 kg corresponds to the approximate median in the population. Body weight test categories (43.2 and 103.3 kg) represent the 5% and 95% percentiles, respectively, in the data set. Vertical dotted lines indicate the [0.80;1.25]-limits.
L1118: Abbreviations: CI, confidence interval; HA, hemophilia A
L1119: **Figure 17. Individually Predicted Average Exposure at Steady State of 0.20 mg/kg Versus Body Weight**
L1120: Source: Modelling Report on Population PK and exposure-response meta-analysis Phase 1, 2 and 3 data Figure 6-3
L1121: ## 8.2. Extrinsic Factors
L1122: No clinical drug-drug interaction (DDI) study was conducted with concizumab. Considering concizumab is a monoclonal antibody, the likelihood of PK-based drug interaction is low.
L1123: In vitro and ex vivo DDI studies were performed with factor products or bypassing agents including rFVIIa, activated prothrombin complex concentrate (aPCC), rFVIII or rFIX in plasma
L1124: from hemophilia patients who were on prophylactic treatment with concizumab. The studies show that thrombin peak is increased in an additive and sometimes synergistic manner with concomitant factor product or bypass agents, indicating pharmacodynamic interaction. While these factor products or bypassing agents can be used in cases of breakthrough bleeds, the following dosing strategies should be implemented as was done in the Phase 3 study by the Applicant:
L1125: - Discontinue treatment with rFVIIa at least 12 hours before starting concizumab
- Discontinue treatment with aPCC at least 48 hours before starting concizumab
- Healthcare providers should discuss with patients receiving concizumab and/or their caregivers the dose and schedule of bypassing agents, if required, while receiving concizumab prophylaxis
L1126: *"Treatment with bypassing agents (e.g., rFVIIa or aPCC) can be used for breakthrough bleeds, and the dose and duration will depend on the location and severity of the bleed. For mild and moderate bleeds that require additional treatment with bypassing agents (e.g., rFVIIa or aPCC), the lowest-approved dose in the approved product labeling is recommended. For aPCC, a maximum dose of 100 U/kg body weight within 24 hours is recommended. For severe bleeds, follow the dosing instructions provided in the approved labeling for the specific product based on clinical judgement."*
L1127: ## 8.3. Plans for Pediatric Drug Development
L1128: ## Animal Data
L1129: Dedicated juvenile animal studies have not been conducted with concizumab as studies in neonatal and infant monkeys are technically challenging and are unlikely to identify potential reactions that were not identified from studies in fully developed (adult) animals. Notable findings in the hemostatic system and immune-related vascular toxicity were observed in adolescent (2 to 3 years of age) and sexually mature (3.5 to 6 years of age) cynomolgus monkeys. The human hemostatic system matures during the early weeks and months of life, and the concentrations of most hemostatic proteins, both in full-term and preterm infants, are comparable to adult values by 6 months of age (Albisetti 2003; Chalmers 2004).
L1130: ## Clinical Data
L1131: Clinical Study 4311, 42 pediatric patients ages 12 to &lt;18 years old, with HAwI or HBwI, were exposed to concizumab either in the main or extension treatment periods. Concizumab has been provided for compassionate use (expanded access) to a total of 14 children and adolescents ages 1 month to &lt;17 years with HBwI up to the cut-off date for the compassionate use report (10 March 2022). Exposure from the compassionate use program has ranged from 1 month to 3 years.
L1132: ## 8.4. Pregnancy, Lactation, and Females/Males of Reproductive Potential
L1133: ## Animal Data
L1134: The following nonclinical information was used in support of the indicated labeling sections and final labeling discussed in Section 23.
L1135: **Table 52. Nonclinical Data Supporting Labeling on Pregnancy and Lactation**
L1136: Labeling Section	Nonclinical Data
Pregnancy	
Dedicated reproductive and developmental toxicology studies were not conducted as concizumab is primarily indicated for use in male patients with hemophilia and the clinical development program was conducted exclusively in males.
Hemophilia A is inherited in an X-linked recessive manner and the disease which occurs predominantly in males (99%) is extremely rare in females. Concizumab is also being indicated for hemophilia B (Factor IX deficiency or Christmas disease, caused by mutations in the Factor IX (FIX) gene on the X chromosome) and hence, is predominantly observed in males (some females who carry the gene may display mild or, rarely, severe symptoms of bleeding).

8.3 Females and Males of Reproductive Potential	
Histopathological evaluations conducted during repeat-dose toxicology studies in adolescent (up to 13 weeks in duration) and sexually mature (up to 52 weeks in duration) male and female cynomolgus monkeys did not elicit any notable effects in reproductive organs.
In mice, inactivation of the TFPI-gene has been shown to result in embryonic lethality (Huang et al. 1997; Ellery et al. 2015) and total TFPI deficiency has not been described in humans (Wood et al. 2014). As placental transfer of IgG, particularly during organogenesis, may differ between mice and humans, the clinical relevance of these findings is unknown (DeSesso et al. 2012).
Recurrent pregnancy loss has been observed in women who exhibited TFPI deficiency in combination with an Activated Protein C (APC) resistance phenotype or in women with anti-TFPI antibodies in combination with antiphospholipid antibodies and suggests that TFPI may be critical to the maintenance of normal pregnancy in humans (Martinuzzo et al. 2005; Gardiner et al. 2006).
The potential risk of blocking TFPI with a monoclonal IgG antibody (e.g., concizumab) in pregnant women is not clear.
L1137: 13.1 Carcinogenesis, Mutagenesis, Impairment of Fertility	
Concizumab is not functional in rodents and standard carcinogenicity studies have not been conducted. Instead, the Applicant conducted a carcinogenicity risk assessment of concizumab using a weight-of-evidence (WOE) approach that considered the mode of action (MOA), a review of relevant literature and an evaluation of the nonclinical and clinical data. It was concluded that treatment with concizumab has the potential to enhance the prothrombotic conditions observed in some cancer patients (as would be expected for any procoagulant compound). However, the assessment did not identify evidence that treatment with concizumab could definitively lead to an increased risk of inducing or enhancing tumor development in humans. The Division determined that no additional nonclinical studies assessing the carcinogenic potential of concizumab were warranted for the indications of hemophilia A and hemophilia B and the CDER Executive Carcinogenicity Assessment Committee (ECAC) concurred with the Division's recommendation.
Genotoxicity studies were not conducted, as concizumab consists of naturally occurring amino acids (endogenous) and is not anticipated to be genotoxic and there were no mechanistic data that suggested a mutagenic potential for concizumab.
Fertility evaluations were included in the 26-week chronic toxicity study conducted in sexually mature male and female cynomolgus monkeys and there were no notable effects on the menstrual cycle duration, the length, width and volume of the testis, total sperm count, sperm motility or sperm morphology in cynomolgus monkeys administered concizumab at doses up to 9 mg/kg/day (SC).
L1138: Source: Reviewer constructed summary table of reproductive toxicology studies submitted to the BLA.
L1139: Abbreviations: APC, Activated Protein C; ECAC, Executive Carcinogenicity Assessment Committee; FIX, Factor IX; IgG, immunoglobulin G; MOA, mode of action; SC, subcutaneous; TFPI, tissue factor pathway inhibitor; WOE, weight-of-evidence.

L1145: ## 8.5. Key Efficacy Review Issues
L1146: ## 8.5.1. Determination If the Pause in Study 4311 Due to Thrombotic Events Impacted the Benefit (ABR)
L1147: ## Issue
L1148: Determination if the pause in Study 4311 due to thrombotic events impacted the benefit (ABR).
L1149: ## Background
L1150: Study 4311 had a concizumab treatment pause for 6 months due to thromboembolic events reported in 3 patients. After the concizumab treatment pause and subsequent restart of Study 4311, a new concizumab dosing regimen was introduced to concizumab PPX arms. The original randomization could have been compromised because more than half of the planned sample size (refer to Table 54) were already enrolled and treated on the original regimen. Four patients randomized before the study pause in the concizumab PPX arm 2 did not restart on the new concizumab dosing regimen.

L1153: Source: Study NN7415-4311 Clinical Study Report, p.118.
Abbreviation: PPX, prophylaxis
L1154: ## Assessment
L1155: As recommended in previous IND meetings, the impact of potential bias introduced by the treatment pause and the robustness of the primary analysis was investigated by the Applicant with four sensitivity analyses. The results from all four sensitivity analyses in Table 55 were confirmed by the FDA statistical reviewer. It was determined that the results of superiority from the primary analysis are robust.
L1156: **Table 55. Sensitivity Analysis for the Primary Efficacy Endpoint: Number of Treated Spontaneous and Traumatic Bleeding Episodes, Study 4311**
L1157: Purpose	Sensitivity Analysis	Result
To investigate the robustness of directly using the bleed data on the initial dosing regimen for patients who were not exposed to the new concizumab regimen	Used multiple imputation to impute the number of treated bleeds on the new concizumab dosing regimen for the 4 patients in arm 2 who did not restart	Estimated ABR ratio (arm 2/arm 1) was 0.14 with 95% CI (0.07, 0.29) and 2-sided p-value <0.001
To investigate the effect of using bleed data on the initial dosing regimen on the treatment effect of concizumab	Used all concizumab exposure data in arm 2 up until the primary analysis cut-off, irrespective of the dosing regimen	Estimated ABR ratio (arm 2/arm 1) was 0.14 with 95% CI (0.07, 0.29) and 2-sided p-value <0.001
To investigate the robustness of a potential superiority conclusion in terms of missing information for patients who were not exposed to the new concizumab regimen	Conducted tipping point analysis for patients only exposed to the initial dosing regimen. Patients only exposed to the initial dosing regimen were modelled with an increasing bleeding rate until the conclusion of superiority is changed	Tipping point was 25, means patients only exposed to the initial dosing regimen need to have 25 more bleed episodes to have non-significant result, which is considered implausible
To investigate a potential difference in treatment effects between patients randomized before and after the pause	Conducted subgroup analysis for patients randomized before and after treatment pause	Estimated ABR ratio (arm 2/arm 1) for patients randomized before the pause was 0.16 with 95% CI (0.08, 0.35)
Estimated ABR ratio (arm 2/arm 1) for patients randomized after restart was 0.06 with 95% CI (0.01, 0.42)
L1158: Source: Study NN7415-4311 Clinical Study Report, Section 9.7.1.2.2 and section 11.2.2; FDA statistical reviewer's analysis.
Abbreviations: ABR, annualized bleeding rate; CI, confidence interval.
L1159: ## Conclusion
L1160: Study 4311 was paused due to thromboembolic events. However, multiple sensitivity analysis confirmed the conclusion of superiority of concizumab PPX over on-demand therapy in reduction in ABR. Despite the study pause, Study 4311 is determined to be an adequate and well controlled study and provides substantial evidence of effectiveness of concizumab for routine prophylaxis to prevent or reduce the frequency of bleeding episodes in adult and adolescent patients with HAwI and HBwI.
L1161: ## 9. Product Quality
L1162: ## Complete Response (CR) With Quality Issues
L1163: Although the Office of Pharmaceutical Quality (OPQ) Review team has assessed BLA 761315 with respect to microbiology and immunogenicity assays and has determined that it meets all of those applicable standards to support the identity, strength, quality, and purity that it purports, the drug substance (DS), drug product (DP) (including device constituent), and facilities disciplines have unresolved quality issues. The deficiencies include inadequate characterization and control
L1164: strategy for [REDACTED] (b) (4) inadequate cell bank stability protocols, inadequate reference material stability protocols, inadequate DS and DP release and stability specifications, and inadequate DS and DP post-approval annual stability protocols. In addition, during the inspection of the [REDACTED] (b) (4) DS manufacturing facility for this application, investigators conveyed deficiencies to the representative of the facility. Therefore, from an OPQ perspective, this BLA is not deemed ready for approval in its present form until the above-mentioned issues are satisfactorily resolved. As such, OPQ recommends a CR action from a product quality perspective.
L1165: ## 9.1. Device or Combination Product Considerations
L1166: CDRH was consulted to assess the proposed concizumab ELISA which is intended for the quantitative measurement of concizumab concentration at a single time point after four weeks from the initiation of treatment with concizumab. The measurement of concizumab concentration is used for a one-time dose adjustment decision based on the Concizumab-ELISA result. CDRH has identified multiple issues with the concizumab ELISA and has determined it is inadequate for postmarket use. See Consult Memo by Dr. Yan Cai for further details.
L1167: CDRH reviewed studies that [REDACTED] (b) (4) conducted to support validation of the [REDACTED] (b) (4) Concizumab ELISA kit for post market sample analysis [REDACTED] (b) (4). The [REDACTED] (b) (4) ELISA will be used for a limited period for post market sample analysis.
L1168: The following comments were communicated to the Applicant at the late cycle meeting on March 15, 2023,
L1169: (b) (4)
L1170: *[To complete this review, detailed instructions are available in the Integrated Review Template How-to Guide.]*
L1171: The Applicant's comments at the late cycle meeting did not adequately address the concerns stated above. CDRH has thus recommended the following comment to be included in the complete response letter (CRL):
L1172: *As stated in the Late-cycle meeting package on March 9, 2023, the validation of the (b) (4) Concizumab ELISA was found inadequate to support the use of the assay in testing clinical specimens. Specifically, for the correlation/cross-validation study, you should include evaluation of a sufficient number of native patient samples to include samples that cover the medical decision levels in order to demonstrate safe use of the (b) (4) Concizumab ELISA as it will be used clinically/for patients. In the precision study, you should include an assessment of instrument-to-instrument and lot-to-lot precision, as multiple instruments and reagent lots will be used clinically and an estimate of device imprecision including these components of precision should be understood prior to device use. For the assay specificity/interference study, you should conduct a dose-response study to determine at which concentrations these substances (hemoglobin, cholesterol, triglyceride, Factor VII (NovoSeven), biotin, Ledipasvir and acetaminophen) no longer interfere with the (b) (4) Concizumab ELISA. You should provide the sample stability data using (b) (4) Concizumab ELISA (b) (4).*
L1173: *Finally, you should provide a root-cause analysis to address the underestimation of concizumab concentrations for contrived samples by the (b) (4) Concizumab ELISA as was seen for specific validation studies (interference, carryover, dilution scheme and sample stability studies).*
