L1552: **Table 95. Study Information, Study 215023, Non-GLP**
L1553: Study Features and Methods	Details
GLP compliance	No
Objective	The report summarizes the results for the dose formulation analysis from the 26 week (211087) and the 52 week (213542) toxicity studies conducted in cynomolgus monkeys. The concizumab employed in these two studies differed in the content of high molecular weight protein (HMWP, i.e., increased dimers and polymers) and the concizumab batch for Study 211087 (26-Weeks) was deliberately enriched with HMWP polymers whereas the batch for Study 213542 (52-weeks) was of the same quality as the clinical study material.
Methods	The percentages of monomers, dimers, and polymers in the concizumab formulations used in the two toxicity studies were measured after storage of concizumab at or below -70°C for the length of the study. A second aliquot (213542) of all concentrations of the dose formulation samples were stored at 5°C for seven days following sampling before being frozen and stored at or below -70°C until analysis. Size exclusion liquid chromatography (SE-HPLC) was employed to determine the content of monomers and HMWPs in the monoclonal antibody samples.
L1554: Source: Reviewer constructed summary table of the toxicology Study (215023) submitted to the BLA

L1555: ## **Assessment of Binding and Internalization of Different Batches of Concizumab by Endothelial Cells (Study CNGU131001, Non-GLP)**
L1556: ## Key Study Findings
L1557: - The binding of concizumab and concizumab multimer batches to endothelial cells was TFPI-specific and concentration-dependent, and the specific binding affinity was found to be comparable (0.3 µg/mL) between the two batches.
- Concizumab and concizumab multimer batches competed for each other's binding to TFPI equally, supporting further that the binding affinities were comparable between these batches.
- A comparable degree (minimal) of cellular internalization was observed between the batches of concizumab (monomeric and multimeric) over time and was found to be specific and saturable.
- The amount of concizumab internalized (specific) by endothelial cells was reduced significantly relative to the amount of cell surface binding (specific) with both the monomeric batch (15-fold lower) and the multimeric batch (20-fold lower) of concizumab and suggests that the cellular target (TFPI) is either internalized minimally/slowly or recycled rapidly to the cell surface.
L1558: *Reviewer Comments: The study indicates that there were no notable differences between the monomeric and multimeric batches of concizumab with regards to binding affinity and cellular internalization by endothelial cells (in vitro), and suggests that aggregates of concizumab are not likely to elicit biological effects different from those of the parent protein (monomeric concizumab).*
L1559: **Table 96. Study Information, Study CNGU131001, Non-GLP**
L1560: Study Features and Methods	Details
GLP compliance	No
Objective	The aim of this study was to evaluate and compare the cellular binding and internalization of two different batches of concizumab containing varying levels of high molecular weight proteins (HMWPs) using vascular endothelial cells.
Methods	One batch of concizumab contained a low level of HMWP (1.4%) and the second batch of concizumab contained a high level of HMWP (93.6%) which was the isolated multimer fraction purified from the batch used in the 26-Week cynomolgus monkey toxicity Study (211187). The two batches of concizumab were iodine (125I)-labeled and assessed in assays (in vitro) designed to assess the direct cellular binding and internalization by an endothelial cell line (EA.hy926) and primary human umbilical vein endothelial cells (HUVEC). The specificity of the binding was determined by an excess addition of unlabeled concizumab.
L1561: Source: Reviewer constructed summary table of the toxicology Study (CNGU131001) submitted to the BLA
L1562: Abbreviations: GLP, good laboratory practices; HMWP, high molecular weight protein; HUVEC, human umbilical vein endothelial cell
L1563: ## 13.2. Individual Reviews of Studies Submitted With the New Drug Application
L1564: All nonclinical studies submitted to the BLA were also submitted and reviewed under the IND.
L1565: ## 14. Clinical Pharmacology
L1566: ## 14.1. In Vitro Studies
L1567: rFVIIa and activated prothrombin complex concentrates (aPCC) are used to treat bleeds in hemophilia patients with inhibitors and may be used for treatment of breakthrough bleeds in patients on concizumab prophylaxis. Likewise, FVIII and FIX are used to treat bleeds in patients with hemophilia A or B without inhibitors. To evaluate the potential for pharmacodynamic interaction between hemostatic agents and concizumab, the following in vitro studies were conducted.
L1568: ## 14.1.1. Drug-Drug Interaction Between Concizumab and rFVIIa, Study 319076
L1569: The effects of concizumab and rFVIIa were quantified in a thrombin generation assay with HA plasma pool and plasma from patients on concizumab prophylaxis in Studies 4310 and 4255.
L1570: - In the HA plasma pool (Figure 18, left), the effects of concizumab and rFVIIa were mainly additive. A statistically significant synergistic effect (p&lt;0.05) was observed for 25nM or 75nM rFVIIa and 1500 ng/mL concizumab. The additive effects accounted for around 85% of the total observed effect for 1500 ng/mL concizumab combined with either 25nM (85.5%) or 75nM (84.7%) rFVIIa (mean values for n = 3).
- rFVIIa increased thrombin peak in plasma from HA patients with inhibitors (Study 4310) or without inhibitors (Study 4255) on concizumab prophylaxis (Figure 18, right). The effects of concizumab and rFVIIa were mainly additive accounting for 60\pm 38\% (25nM rFVIIa, Study 4255, mean and standard deviation (SD) of data from 28 subjects), 67 \pm 8\% (75nM rFVIIa, Study 4255, mean and SD of data from 24 subjects) and 78 \pm 14\% (75nM rFVIIa, Study 4310, mean and SD of data from 15 subjects) of the total observed effects.
L1571: Overall, a significant synergistic effect between rFVIIa and concizumab was observed in plasma accounting for up to 40% of the total observed effect.
L1572: **Figure 18.** Thrombin Peak After Adding rFVIIa and Concizumab In Vitro to a Hemophilia A Plasma Pool or Ex Vivo to Plasma Samples From Hemophilia A Patients With Inhibitors on Concizumab Prophylaxis
L1573: Source: 2.6.2 Pharmacology Written Summary Figure 18
L1574: Note: The grey area depicts (A) the mean \pm 1 SD of thrombin peak observed upon addition of normal level of rFVIII (1 IU/mL) to the hemophilia A (HA) plasma pool (n=3) or (B) the mean and 95% CI of rFVIII (1 IU/mL) added to noninhibitor hemophilia A plasma (Trial NN7415-4255, n=30 baseline samples). The left datapoint on both graphs depicts the effect of rFVIIa in the absence of concizumab.
L1575: Abbreviations: HA, hemophilia A; FVIII, factor VIII; rFVIIa, recombinant factor VIIa
L1576: ## 14.1.2. Drug-Drug Interaction Between Concizumab and aPCC, Study 319076
L1577: Drug interactions between concizumab and aPCC was evaluated in thrombin generation assay. aPCC enhanced the thrombin peak both in the absence and presence of concizumab in a HA plasma pool (Figure 19, left) and in plasma from HA patients with inhibitors (Study 4310) and without inhibitors (Study 4255) on concizumab prophylaxis (Figure 19, right).
L1578: - The effect of concizumab and aPCC were mainly additive accounting for between 83% (150 ng/mL concizumab combined with 1 U/mL aPCC) and 67% (1500 ng/mL concizumab combined with 0.5 U/mL aPCC) of the total observed effects in the plasma pool.
- In the patient samples, the additive effects of concizumab and 1 U/mL aPCC corresponded to 80 \pm 22\% of the total observed effects (Study 4310, mean and SD of data from 15 subjects). No significant synergistic effect was observed in Study 4255.
L1579: Overall, the data demonstrated that aPCC enhanced the thrombin generation in plasma containing concizumab and that the effect of concizumab and aPCC were mainly additive with a significant synergistic effect of up to 33% of the total effect.

The left datapoint on both graphs depicts the effect of aPCC in the absence of concizumab.

L1584: ## 14.1.3. Drug-Drug Interaction Between Concizumab and rFVIII, Study 321538
L1585: rFVIII was added to an HA plasma pool alone or together with concizumab (Figure 20, left) and to 33 plasma samples from 8 patients on concizumab prophylaxis, where free TFPI levels (TFPI not in complex with concizumab) were below detection limit of the assay (Figure 20, right). For
L1586: comparison, rFVIII was added to pre-dose plasma samples available for seven of these patients. rFVIII increased the thrombin peak both in the absence and presence of concizumab. In the plasma pool, a statistically significant synergistic effect was observed for 1 IU/mL rFVIII combined with 1500, 4500 and 15000 ng/mL concizumab, for 0.5 IU/mL rFVIII combined with 1500 and 4500 ng/mL concizumab and for 0.25 IU/mL rFVIII combined with 4500 and 15000 ng/mL concizumab. In these cases, the additive effects of concizumab and rFVIII corresponded to between 78% (1 IU/mL rFVIII combined with 4500 ng/mL concizumab) and 90% (0.25 IU/mL rFVIII combined with 15000 ng/mL concizumab) of the total observed effects. In patient plasma, no significant synergistic effect between rFVIII and concizumab was observed. Thus, when rFVIII was combined with concizumab, between 0 and 22% of the total effect was caused by a synergistic effect.
L1587: **Figure 20. Thrombin Peak After Adding rFVIII and Concizumab In Vitro to a HA Plasma Pool or FVIII Ex Vivo to Plasma Samples From Hemophilia A Patients Concizumab Prophylaxis**
L1588: Source: 2.6.2 Pharmacology Written Summary Figure 20

Data for 1500 ng/mL concizumab is shown in A (open squares), while data for other concentrations are available in Study ID 321538.

L1591: ## 14.1.4. Drug-Drug Interaction Between Concizumab and rFIX, Study 321538
L1592: rFIX was added to a HB plasma pool alone or together with concizumab. rFIX enhanced the thrombin peak in HB plasma both in the absence and presence of concizumab (Figure 21). Drug interaction was observed for 1 IU/mL rFIX combined with concizumab at 1500 ng/mL, 4500 ng/mL and 15000 ng/mL and for 0.5 IU/mL rFIX combined with 100nM concizumab. The additive effects of concizumab and 1 IU/mL rFIX corresponded to between 111% (1500 ng/mL concizumab) and 113% (15000 ng/mL concizumab) of the total observed effects, and the additive effects of 0.5 IU/mL rFIX and 15000 ng/mL concizumab corresponded to 112% of the total observed effects.
L1593: Overall, the data suggest an additive effect when combining rFIX with concizumab.

L1598: ## 14.2. In Vivo Studies
L1599: ## 14.2.1. Single Ascending Dose in Healthy and Hemophilia Subjects, Study 3813
L1600: ## Study Design
L1601: This is a randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, single-dose, dose-escalation study investigating safety, pharmacokinetics, and pharmacodynamics of concizumab administered intravenously and subcutaneously to healthy male subjects and patients with hemophilia (HA or HB). A total of 52 subjects (28 healthy subjects and 24 hemophilia subjects) were randomized and completed the study. The subjects were randomized in a 3:1 manner to receive concizumab or placebo. The studied doses are listed below:
L1602: - Intravenous administrationHealthy subjects (12): 0.5, 5, 50, 250 \mug/kg
- Hemophilia patients (9 HA, 3 HB): 250, 1000, 3000, 9000 mg/kg
- Placebo (4 healthy subjects and 4 hemophilia patients)
- Subcutaneous administrationHealthy subjects (9): 50, 250, 1000 mg/kg
- Hemophilia patients (6 HA): 1000, 3000 mg/kg
- Placebo (3 healthy subjects and 2 hemophilia patients)
L1603: Serial PK and PD samples were collected at pre-dose, 0, 5, 15 and 30 minutes, 1, 4, 8, 12, 24, 36, and 48 hours post-dosing, at each study visit till the end of study (i.e., up to 43 days after dosing). Validated ELISA and chromogenic assay were used for the PK and PD assessment, respectively.
L1604: ## **Results**
L1605: Concizumab concentration-time profiles following IV and SC administration are presented in Figure 22 with PK parameters summarized in Table 97 and Table 98.
L1606: - Across dose levels, concizumab exposure (maximum plasma concentration (C_{max}) and area under the concentration-time curve) increased with increasing dose in a greater than dose proportional manner following IV and SC administration. For example, there was a 23-fold decrease in mean clearance with a 12-fold increase in concizumab doses (i.e., 250 to 3000 \mu\text{g}/\text{kg}). The non-linear PK was considered to be mainly due to target-mediated drug disposition, which is a common characteristic of monoclonal antibodies.
- PK profiles were similar between healthy subjects and hemophilia patients and between HA and HB patients.
- The median t_{max} across dose cohorts ranged from 0.5 to 2.9 days following SC administration.
- Following a single dose IV injection of 0.25 \text{mg}/\text{kg}, the half-life was about 26 to 31 hours.
L1607: Mean plasma profiles for free tissue factor pathway inhibitor (free TFPI, the PD marker) concentrations following IV and SC administration of concizumab to healthy subjects and patients with hemophilia are presented in Figure 23 and Figure 24.
L1608: - The free TFPI levels decreased with increasing concizumab doses following both IV and SC administration in both healthy subjects and hemophilia patients. Duration of free TFPI suppression increased with increasing concizumab dose. Following a single IV administration, the free TFPI level was completely suppressed for approximately 12 hours and a week for 0.25 \text{mg}/\text{kg} and 3 \text{mg}/\text{kg}, respectively.
L1609: Concizumab was well-tolerated and there were no deaths or other serious adverse events observed in this study.

L1632: ## Reviewer's Comments:
L1633: - *The non-linear elimination of concizumab seems to be due to the target-mediated drug disposition (TMDD) following binding of concizumab to endothelial cell-anchored TFPI (the target) with subsequent endocytosis and degradation (target-mediated clearance). In addition to the drug-target complex elimination pathway, concizumab is also eliminated in a linear fashion, as other monoclonal antibodies, mainly by catabolism with a half-life of approximately 3 weeks.*
- *TFPI is present on endothelial cells (~92%), platelets (~1%) and in plasma (~7%) (Broze and Girard 2012). Concizumab can readily access the TFPI on the surface of endothelial cells (mTFPI) and the free soluble TFPI circulating in the plasma (sTFPI). While both sTFPI and the TFPI in circulating platelets can migrate to the sites of vessel injury and inhibit coagulation, sTFPI is used as an exploratory pharmacodynamics (PD) marker for assessing hemophilia treatment due to its ease of sampling.*
- *Nonlinear PK of concizumab was observed after IV and SC dosing in healthy volunteers and patients, reflecting dose-dependent, target-mediated clearance at low doses. The*
L1634: *exact clearance mechanisms at each phase of elimination are not clear. The current hypothesis from the Applicant is that when drug concentrations are high enough to saturate targets, the rate of drug elimination is governed by linear nonspecific routes, together with a fixed rate of target mediated elimination. When drug concentrations go lower, the targets are not all saturated and both nonspecific and target-mediated elimination routes are important, resulting in nonlinear PK. When drug concentrations are even lower such that targets are not saturated, the target mediated elimination becomes the main route of elimination and PK is linear. Evaluation of the concentration-time profile provides an estimate of drug concentrations associated with each of these phases. A change in the slope of the concentration-time curve during the elimination phase, from shallow (target saturated; linear nonspecific routes govern elimination) to steep (targets not all saturated; combination of linear and nonlinear routes govern elimination), and then again from steep to shallow (targets not saturated; linear routes govern elimination) will be apparent for profiles that have high enough concentrations to fully saturate the target receptor and PK sampling sufficient to capture the phase when concentrations fall well below the threshold.*
L1635: ## 14.2.2. Single Dose Study in Healthy Japanese Subjects, Study 3981
L1636: ## Study Design
L1637: This was a randomized, placebo-controlled, double-blind, single-dose study to assess the PK of SC administered concizumab in healthy male Japanese subjects. A total of 8 subjects received a single SC dose of concizumab (250 µg/kg or 1000 µg/kg) or placebo. In each dose cohort, 3 subjects received active treatment and 1 subject received placebo. Blood sampling was done prior to dosing and up to 33 days post-dosing for assessment of PK, PD, and laboratory safety parameters.
L1638: ## Study Results
L1639: Mean concizumab concentration-time profiles are presented in Figure 25. Derived PK endpoints are presented in Table 99.

L1657: ## 14.2.3. Multiple Dose Study in Healthy Subjects, Study 3986
L1658: ## Study Design
L1659: This was an open-label, multiple-dosing study investigating safety, PK, and PD of concizumab administered subcutaneously to healthy male subjects and hemophilia subjects. The study was planned to include 3 ascending dose tiers: 250 \mug/kg (healthy subjects), 500 \mug/kg (hemophilia subjects) or 1000 \mug/kg (hemophilia subjects) every other day (EOD) 8 times. At each dose tier,
L1660: four subjects were planned to be included. Because of premature end of the study,3 only the low dose tier (250 µg/kg EOD 8 times) in 4 healthy subjects was included in this study. PK and PD samples were collected at pre-dose and 1 hour post-dose at each study visit and up to 35 days post-dosing.
L1661: ## **Study Results**
L1662: - The mean trough level (C_{\text{trough}}) prior to the last SC dose administration (Day 15) was 66.1 ng/mL, ranging from 50 to 75 ng/mL (Figure 28).
- Free TFPI plasma levels decreased from baseline during the concizumab treatment period (Figure 29). The mean free TFPI plasma levels were reduced by 68.1% from 79.9 ng/mL at baseline to 25.5 ng/mL on the last day of concizumab treatment (Day 15) and had returned to baseline values at the end-of-study visit.
- No anti-concizumab antibodies were detected during the study.
- There were no deaths or serious adverse events observed in this study.

L1664: Source: Study Report 3986 Figure 11-1
L1665: Abbreviations: ELISA, enzyme-linked immunoassay; SD, standard deviation
L1666: 3 During the conduct of the study, nonclinical safety findings were seen in the nonclinical program for concizumab, which had to be investigated before exposing further human subjects to the study product. Therefore, the study was set on hold and a decision to prematurely end the study was subsequently made.

L1670: ## 14.2.4. Multiple Dose Study in Hemophilia Patients, Study 4159
L1671: ## **Study Design**
L1672: This was a randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, multiple-dose, dose-escalation study in 18 patients with hemophilia A without inhibitors. Three dose levels of concizumab (0.25, 0.5, 0.8 mg/kg) were administered SC every fourth days for 12 times over a 42-day period. However, to rapidly reach steady state at the targeted plasma concentration, the first two doses were given on two consecutive days. All study products were administered to the study subjects at the clinic under the surveillance of medically trained study site staff. At each dose level, eight patients were randomized in a 3: 1 ratio to receive concizumab or placebo treatment. Blood samples for PK and PD assessment were collected pre-dose at each day of dose administration, 4 hours after administration on Days 18 and 42, and 48 hours after the last dose on Day 42.
L1673: ## **Study Results**
L1674: - Non-linear PK of concizumab was observed in HA patients. Relative to the 0.25 mg/kg group, geometric mean AUCt (planned Day 38-42) values were 3-fold higher in the 0.50 mg/kg group and nearly 11-fold higher in the 0.80 mg/kg group (Figure 30 and Table 100).
- There was a high between-subject variability following high doses of concizumab.
- A clear PD response to concizumab dosing was observed as the concentration of free TFPI in plasma was inversely correlated with that of concizumab. Mean trough levels of free plasma TFPI was reduced by ~27%, 55% and 75% from baseline for the 0.25, 0.5 and 0.80 mg/kg groups, respectively (Figure 31).
- No ADAs (binding or neutralizing) were detected during this study.

L1689: **Reviewer's Comments:** The study aimed to include five concizumab dose levels, but was stopped after three due to a safety committee decision not to dose-escalate to the fourth concizumab dose level of 1.1 mg/kg, i.e., this study was finalized following the completion of the 0.80 mg/kg group. Blinded preliminary safety and PK/PD data from the 0.80 mg/kg group was reviewed by the safety committee. Marked changes in coagulation parameters were observed, including a decrease from baseline in fibrinogen, and a pronounced increase in D-dimer and F1 + 2 outside of normal range. In addition, a substantial between-subject variation in pro-coagulant response to concizumab was observed. Based on this, the safety committee decided not to proceed to the 1.1 mg/kg concizumab dose level.
L1690: - In this study, concizumab was given at doses of 0.25 mg/kg, 0.5 mg/kg and 0.8 mg/kg on Day 1, Day 2 and every 3 days afterward. As shown in Figure 30, slope of the PK curve during the elimination phase was steeper for the 0.8 and 0.5 mg/kg doses than that for the 0.25 mg/kg dose. It is consistent with the observation that non-linear PK due to TMDD plays a dominant role in the target concentration range, leading to a much faster drug clearance at these concentrations. In addition, higher between-subject and within-subject variability were observed at the higher doses of concizumab (Table 101). Given a significant concentration-dependent clearance due to TMDD, once-daily dosing may minimize within-patient PK variability by minimizing the peak-to-trough differences.

L1706: ## 14.2.5. Efficacy and Safety Study in Adult and Adolescent Patients With Hemophilia, Study 4311, After Restart
L1707: ## **Study Design**
L1708: This was a randomized, open-label study to evaluate the efficacy and safety of daily concizumab prophylaxis (PPX) administered SC in patients with HA or HB with inhibitors. Initially, patients
L1709: were randomized to concizumab PPX (arm 2) or no PPX (arm 1) or assigned to non-randomized PPX arms (arms 3 or 4) based on their treatment regimen before entering the study. After the restart of the study, patients continued in the treatment arm which they were initially randomized or assigned to. New eligible patients were randomized to concizumab PPX (arm 2) or no PPX (arm 1) or assigned to arm 4. The study consisted of a 3-week screening period, a main part (24-32 week treatment period), an extension part (128-136 week treatment period), and a 7-week follow-up period (Figure 33).
L1710: Upon restart of the study, the dosing regimen was a loading dose of 1.0 mg/kg concizumab SC on the first day of treatment, followed by an initial daily dose of 0.20 mg/kg concizumab. Within the initial 5 to 8 week maintenance dose setting period on 0.20 mg/kg concizumab, the dose was either increased to 0.25 mg/kg if the concizumab exposure was below 200 ng/mL, decreased to 0.15 mg/kg if the exposure was above 4000 ng/mL or maintained at 0.20 mg/kg if exposure was between 200-4000 ng/mL. The dose setting was based on the concizumab exposure level after 4 weeks of concizumab exposure at visit 4a. The investigational drug product consisted of the drug constituent (concizumab 40 mg/mL and 100 mg/mL), and a device constituent (a (b) (4) pen-injector).
L1711: For patients on concizumab PPX (patients in arms 2, 3 and 4), two 24-hour PK/PD-sessions were conducted at visits 2a and 9a. The concizumab dose was administered in the abdomen and blood samples were drawn pre-dosing, 3, 6, 9 and 24 hours after dosing. In addition to the two PK/PD sessions, pre-dose trough concizumab plasma concentration (C_{\text{trough}}) was measured throughout the concizumab treatment period.
L1712: **Figure 33. Study Scheme, Study 4311, After Restart**
L1713: Source: Study NN7415-4311 Clinical Study Report, Figure 9-1 (p.42)
Abbreviations: OnD, on demand; PPX, prophylaxis; V, Visit
L1714: ## Study Results
L1715: - The concizumab plasma concentrations measured at Week 4 were used to determine the maintenance dose for each patient following an initial dose of 0.20 mg/kg. One (1) patient (1.0%) had a concizumab plasma concentration &gt;4000 ng/mL prior to maintenance dose setting and was decreased to a maintenance dose of 0.15 mg/kg. Twenty-four (24) patients (24.7%) had concizumab plasma concentrations &lt;200 ng/mL prior to maintenance dose
L1716: setting and were increased to a maintenance dose of 0.25 mg/kg. Seventy-two patients (74.2%) remained on a maintenance dose of 0.20 mg/kg.
L1717: - At Week 24, the geometric mean (CV%) C_{\text{trough}} concentration of concizumab was 665.4 (220%) ng/mL in 94 patients who received concizumab treatment. The median concentration was 786 ng/mL (Figure 34).
- Geometric mean plots for the 24-hour profiles on Day 1 and at Week 24 are presented in Figure 35. The geometric mean (CV%) C_{\text{max}} values were 383 (410%) on Day 1 and 1167.1 (270%) ng/mL and at Week 24. The mean (SD) AUC_t value were 12870 (16705) ng\cdoth/mL on Day 1 and 33784 (31052) ng\cdoth at Week 24. The mean C_{\text{max}}/C_{\text{trough}} ratio at Week 24 was 2.2.
- The geometric mean pre-dose free TFPI levels decreased from 88.3 ng/mL at baseline to 10.7 ng/mL at Week 24 for patients on concizumab PPX (arms 2-4). After the initial decrease, the levels remained stable over time. Patients on no PPX (arm 1) maintained baseline levels for free TFPI (Figure 36).
- The geometric mean pre-dose thrombin peak levels were increased from 13.5 nmol/L at baseline to 105.4 nmol/L (within range of normal plasma) at Week 24 for patients on concizumab PPX (arms 2-4). After the initial increase, the levels remained stable over time. Patients on no PPX (arm 1) maintained baseline thrombin peak levels (Figure 37).

L1731: ## 14.3. Bioanalytical Method Validation and Performance
L1732: ## 14.3.1. Bioanalytical Method for PK Assessment
L1733: (b) (4)
L1734: ## 14.3.2. Bioanalytical Method for PD Assessment
L1735: Validated PD assays were used to measure the levels of free TFPI (not bound to concizumab), and thrombin generation in plasma.
L1736: ## Free TFPI
L1737: Free TFPI in plasma, i.e., TFPI not bound to concizumab, was quantified using a commercially available ELISA kit (Asserachrom® Total TFPI, Diagnostica Stago, Asnières-sur-Seine, France). The assay uses 2 monoclonal anti-TFPI antibodies where the catching antibody competes with concizumab resulting in specific detection of TFPI not bound to concizumab. Validation parameters for free TFPI are presented below in Table 107.
L1738: **Table 107. Validation Parameters for Free TFPI**
L1739: Validation parameter	(b) (4)
Calibrator	3.2% citrated human plasma with a known concentration of TFPI
LLOQ	12.5 ng/mL	17.1 ng/mL	9.6 ng/mL
Precision	≤ 20%
Stability at RT	6 hours
Long-time stability	12 months at -80°C
Validation Study ID	211073	(b) (4) 240159	(b) (4) -2015-009
L1740: Source: Summary of biopharmaceutical studies and associated analytical methods Table 1-5
L1741: Abbreviations: LLOQ, lower limit of quantification; RT, room temperature; TFPI, tissue factor pathway inhibitor
L1742: ## Thrombin Generation
L1743: Thrombin generation is a process that determines the extent of the thrombin burst which is crucial for the formation of a stable fibrin clot. The TG assay (TGA) was an exploratory assay for a global measurement of the potential of platelet-poor plasma to support hemostasis. The assay is validated as an exploratory biomarker with regards to concizumab interference and precision.

L1751: ## 14.4. Immunogenicity Assessment—Impact of PK/PD, Efficacy, and Safety
L1752: Development of anti-concizumab antibodies was investigated in all clinical studies (Phase 1 single dose studies 3813 and 3981, Phase 1 multiple dose studies 3986 and 4159, Phase 2 multiple dose studies 4310 and 4255, and Phase 3 multiple dose Study 4311). ADA samples were collected with time-matched PK samples. The sampling intervals in each of the clinical studies are shown in Table 109.

L1757: Abbreviation: ADA, anti-drug antibody
L1758: No anti-concizumab antibodies (ADAs were detected in Phase 1 studies. Of the 185 patients treated with concizumab in the multiple-dose studies throughout the clinical development program (Studies 4159, 4310, 4255 and 4311 up until PACO), 25.4% (47 patients) tested positive for binding anti-concizumab antibodies at one or more visits after first concizumab dose. Twelve patients (6.5%) tested positive for anti-concizumab antibodies with in vitro neutralizing effects (Table 110).

L1764: Abbreviations: ADA, anti-drug antibody; HA, hemophilia A; HAwI, hemophilia A with inhibitors; HBwI, hemophilia B with inhibitors; N, number of patients
L1765: Of the 47 patients testing positive for ADAs, binding anti-concizumab antibody titers were generally low. Apart from 1 patient with medium level titers and 1 patient with high titers, for 96% of ADA-positive patients, titers ranged from 100 to 6400 (equals 1-64 without the MRD factored).
L1766: - Medium-level ADA titers: One ADA-positive patient (1/47; 2%) had a maximum ADA titer at 25600. This patient had anti-concizumab antibody titers declined to 12800 after 24 weeks, where they stayed constant until the data cut-off for this summary.
- High-level ADA titers: One patient (subject ID [REDACTED](b)(6)) first tested positive for ADAs (titer 1600) about 12 weeks, peaked at 12800, and returned to titer 1600 at the Week 32. The patient discontinued concizumab treatment in Week 33 due to surgery after an accident leading to bone fracture in the hand. Following the surgery, the patient experienced hemorrhagic shock after arterial puncture during blood sampling. The patient restarted concizumab treatment at week 40. ADA titers had increased to medium levels (51200) at Week 40 to high levels (204800) at Week 56 and 64, likely caused by a combination of a boosted immune response after the traumatic events experienced by the patient and re-initiation of concizumab treatment.
L1767: It should be noted ADA and neutralizing antibody assays were not considered fully validated by OBP reviewers. See Complete Response (CR) letter for comments related to immunogenicity assays.
L1768: ## **Impact of Immunogenicity on Concizumab Exposure (PK)**
L1769: For almost all patients (except for subject ID [REDACTED](b)(6)) positive for anti-concizumab antibodies in Phase 2 and 3 studies, the presence of anti-concizumab antibodies, some with *in vitro*-neutralizing effects, appeared to not affect concizumab exposure:
L1770: - In both Phase 2 and Phase 3, concizumab exposure over time after first dose was similar between patients positive and negative for anti-concizumab antibodies (Figure 38 and Figure
L1771: 39). Seroconversion, or occurrence of anti-concizumab antibodies with in vitro-neutralizing effects, did not appear to cause a change in exposure.

- For the patient with a high titer level (subject ID (b) (6)), per Applicant, it was not possible to determine whether the high antibody titers reduced concizumab exposure, since plasma concizumab concentration measurements failed, likely due to ADA interference in the PK assay. It is still likely that ADAs increased concizumab clearance leading to decreased drug concentrations, as the PD marker free TFPI returned to the baseline (see next section).
L1772: Figure 38. Concizumab Plasma Concentration Over Time – ADA-Positive Versus – Negative Patients (Top Plot: Study 4310 and Bottom Plot: Study 4255)

Abbreviation: ADA, anti-drug antibody

L1775: Source: Integrated summary of immunogenicity Figure 7.1.13 and Figure 7.1.14
Abbreviation: ADA, anti-drug antibody
L1776: ## **Impact of Immunogenicity on Free TFPI (PD)**
L1777: The presence of anti-concizumab antibodies, some with in vitro-neutralizing effects, did not appear to generally affect the ability of concizumab to bind TFPI.
L1778: - In both Phase 2 and Phase 3, free TFPI profiles were similar between patients positive and negative for anti-concizumab antibodies (Figure 40 and Figure 41). Seroconversion did not appear to cause a general change in free TPFI levels in any but one of the anti-concizumab antibody-positive patients.
- A patient in Study 4310 (Subject ID [REDACTED](b) (6)) experienced free TFPI return to baseline while he was positive for high titer binding ADAs and for ADAs with in vitro-neutralizing effects.

L1784: ## Reviewer's Comments:
L1785: - *It should be noted that Phase 2 studies 4310, 4255 and Phase 3 Study 4311 before restart had different dosing regimens compared to that in Study 4311 after restart, which was the pivotal Phase 3 study to support the safety and efficacy of concizumab for the proposed indication. Considering that dose, dosing frequency, and duration of treatment are all factors that can affect immunogenicity of a therapeutic protein, the ADA assessment should mainly focus on data from 4311 after restart. Nonetheless, current*
L1786: data show that the incidence of ADAs, neutralizing antibodies, as well as the impact on PK and PD were comparable across these clinical trials.
L1787: - Given the large inter-subject variability observed in the PK and PD profiles, comparisons between ADA positive and negative patients may be not sufficiently sensitive to determine the impact of ADAs on the PK and PD of concizumab. Therefore, the impact of ADA was also evaluated by comparing concizumab PK and PD before and after ADA formation in the individuals who were ADA positive. As shown in Figure 42, the within-subject comparison also supports a lack of impact on concizumab PK and PD due to ADAs.The presence of ADAs, some with in vitro-neutralizing effects, appeared to not affect concizumab exposure or the ability of concizumab to bind TFPI.
- One patient (Subject ID [REDACTED](b) (6)) in Figure 42 (highlighted with a dotted blue line) showed altered PK/PD (i.e., decreased concizumab concentration and increased free TFPI concentration) after ADA development. This patient had not been treated with concizumab for approximately one month when he tested positive for ADAs on [REDACTED](b) (6). Accordingly, this patient had a single measurement of low concizumab concentration (17.3 ng/mL) and a corresponding high TFPI (100.5 ng/mL) at the same visit. His concizumab concentration went up to 798 ng/mL, and free TFPI went down to 20.7 ng/mL at the next visit. This patient did not have in vitro-neutralizing ADAs.
L1788: **Figure 42. Geometric Mean Concizumab Trough and Free TFPI Concentrations Before and After First ADA-positive Sample in Individual Patients From Study 4311 Up Until Primary Analysis Cut-off***
L1789: Source: Applicant's response to Information Request sent on December 20th, 2022
L1790: * Data from patients with at least one PK (concizumab trough concentration) or PD (free TFPI) measurement before the first ADA positive sample and at least one PK/PD measurement afterwards were included. This was to make sure that the individual patients had data both before and after ADA development to facilitate the within-subject comparison. Based on the above criteria, data from 16 patients in Study 4311 (after restart) were included in the PK (concizumab trough concentration) plot and data from 13 patients were included in the PD (free TFPI) plot.
L1791: Abbreviations: ADA, anti-drug antibody; PD, pharmacodynamics; PK, pharmacokinetics; TFPI, tissue factor pathway inhibitor
L1792: ## 14.5. Pharmacometrics Assessment
L1793: ## 14.5.1. Summary of Applicant's Population PK Analysis
L1794: The population PK (PPK) model was developed based on all Phase 1 and 2 studies, and the Phase 3 study 4311 in patients with hemophilia A and B with inhibitors. The proposed concizumab regimen (applied in Phase 3) includes a loading dose of 1 mg/kg once on Day 1, and from Day 2 until individual maintenance dose setting, once daily dosing of 0.20 mg/kg. Four weeks after initiation of treatment, concizumab plasma concentration is measured and based on this exposure level, the maintenance dose is set:
L1795: - &lt;200 ng/mL: 0.25 mg/kg once daily
- 200 to 4000 ng/mL: 0.20 mg/kg once daily
- &gt;4000 ng/mL: 0.15 mg/kg once daily
L1796: The PPK analysis was conducted using 4145 evaluable concizumab concentrations in a total of 232 patients. The concizumab dose level ranges included from 0.0005 to 9 mg/kg administered by IV and 0.05 to 3 mg/kg SC injection. The summary of studies used for PPK analysis are listed in Table 111. Summary statistics of the continuous and categorical covariates that were evaluated in the population PK analysis are shown in Table 112.

L1809: The PK of concizumab was best characterized by a 2-compartment model with combined linear clearance and target mediated drug disposition (TMDD), together with a transit compartment for delayed absorption. Of note, the TMDD was estimated using a quasi-steady-state approximation, which assumes an instant equilibrium between concizumab and TFPI. The final concizumab PK model parameter estimates, and the corresponding 95% confidence intervals (CIs) are presented in Table 113. Due to sparse PK profiles in the Phase 3, data from previous Phase 1 &amp; 2 was used for estimation of PK parameters, then PK parameters were fixed. The only parameter - the total capacity TMDD parameter R_{tot} and covariate effects were then re-estimated using data from Phase 3 Study 4311.

L1824: ## Exposure with Body Weights
L1825: PPK simulation results showed the mean concizumab exposure evidently increases with increasing body weight in Study 4311 (Figure 46). In addition, using a flat dose of 15 mg (e.g., a
L1826: typical subject with a body weight around 75 kg, 0.20 mg/kg corresponds to 15 mg), the simulation results showed remarkably high exposure for subjects with low body weight and low exposure for subjects with high body weight (Figure 47). This is likely caused by the nonlinear PK due to TMDD. If necessary, a body weight banded dosing approach (either with flat or per kg dosing) could be considered to further reduce exposure variability. Nonetheless, after 4 weeks of treatment, patients with low or high exposure should adjust dose to 0.25 or 0.15 mg/kg, respectively, resulting in more similar exposure across patients.

Points with bars are geometric mean and 95% CI of the individually predicted average exposure at steady state of 0.20 mg/kg. The solid line represents the model-predicted average exposure at steady state of 0.20 mg/kg versus body weight for the typical subjects.

L1837: ## **Simulation for Maintenance-Dose Setting**
L1838: The proposed concizumab regimen (applied in Phase 3) includes a loading dose of 1 mg/kg once on Day 1, and from Day 2 until individual maintenance dose setting, once daily dosing of 0.20 mg/kg. Four weeks after initiation of treatment, concizumab plasma concentration is measured and based on this exposure level, the maintenance dose is set:
L1839: - &lt;200 ng/mL: 0.25 mg/kg once daily
- 200 to 4000 ng/mL: 0.20 mg/kg once daily
- &gt;4000 ng/mL: 0.15 mg/kg once daily
L1840: Simulations support that maintenance dose setting reduced the total, population-level variability in concizumab exposure (Figure 48). Specifically, there was a marginal increase in exposure at the low end and a decrease in exposure at the high end (Table 114).
L1841: Figure 48. Simulated Exposure Without (A) and With (B) Maintenance Dose Setting
L1842: A
L1843: B
L1844: Individual concentration-time profiles (without RUV) were simulated based on the final model assuming a body weight distribution similar to Trial 4311 (10,000 subjects simulated). Shaded area represents the 90% prediction interval, and dark line represents the geometric mean exposure levels. Maintenance dose setting was implemented as a dose increase or decrease at week 6, based on C_{\text{trough}} values (including RUV) occurring at week 4.
L1845: Source: Population PK Study Report Modeling Report – Phase 3a, Figure 8-1.
L1846: Abbreviation: C_{\text{trough}}, trough concentration; RUV, residual urine volume

L1852: ## Exposure in Pediatric Patients
L1853: In Study 4311, individual steady-state exposure estimates for adolescents (12 to 17 years) were overlapping with exposure estimates in adults (&gt;18 years) when treated with 0.20 mg/kg concizumab (Figure 49).
L1854: **Figure 49. Individually Predicted Average Exposure at Steady State of 0.20 mg/kg for Adolescents and Adults in Study 4311**

L1856: Source: Population PK Study Report Modeling Report – Phase 3a, Figure 8-2.
L1857: Abbreviations: C_{avg}, average plasma concentration; CI, confidence interval; N, number of subjects
L1858: (b) (4)
L1859: *Reviewer's Comments: The Applicant's PPK model appears adequate to describe the concizumab serum concentration-time profiles in the observed range following the administration of concizumab in patients with hemophilia A and B with inhibitors from Phase 3 Study 4311. The shrinkage value was 9.05% for IIV on R_{tot}, indicating there was no obvious bias in the parameter estimates. Therefore, the PK model is acceptable for simulating post hoc exposure metrics, e.g., C_{avg} and \bar{C}_{avg, trial} of concizumab for exposure-response (E-R) analyses for efficacy and safety measurements.*
L1860: *Covariate analysis identified two statistically significant covariates: body weight (27-130.7 kg), and Study 4311 on R_{tot}. Allometric scaling was also applied to CL and volume terms (V_c, V_p) using fixed exponents (0.75 and 1.00 respectively) to account for PK variability related to body weight in the PPK model. In addition, the other covariates such as age (12 to 79 years), race*
L1861: (White (62.9%), Asian (25%), Black or African American (6%), American Indian or Alaska Native (1.3%), other (1.3%)), Ethnicity (Hispanic or Latino (6%), not Hispanic or Latino (91%)) were not identified as significant covariate for the concizumab exposure.
L1862: The significant impact of body weight on concizumab PK supported the body-weight-based dosing regimen:
L1863: - The concizumab exposure increases with increasing body weight.
- Using a flat dose (e.g., 15 mg) will result high exposure for subjects with low body weight and low exposure for subjects with high body weight.
L1864: However, the mean concizumab exposure still evidently increases with increasing body weight (with a ~5 fold exposure increase for body weight increasing from 40 kg to 100 kg) despite the per-kilogram dosing. This is likely caused by the nonlinear PK due to TMDD. If necessary, a body weight banded dosing approach (either with flat or per kilogram dosing) could be considered to further reduce exposure variability. Nonetheless, after 4 weeks of treatment, patients with low or high exposure should adjust dose to 0.25 or 0.15 mg/kg, respectively, resulting in more similar exposure across patients.
L1865: In addition, PPK simulations further supported maintenance dose setting with an increase in exposure at the low end and a decrease in exposure at the high end, and almost all patients were above the 200 ng/mL concentration level: concentrations at the plateau of effect predicted by exposure-response (E-R) for annualized bleeding rate.
L1866: Additional simulation with steady-state exposure estimates for adolescents (12 to 17 years) enrolled in Study 4311 were overlapping with exposure estimates in adults (&gt;18 years) when treated with 0.20 mg/kg concizumab, which indicated no significant effect of age (adolescents [12 to 17 years] versus adults [\ge18 years]).(b) (4)
L1867: High inter- and intra-subject variability in plasma concizumab concentrations following high doses of concizumab was observed in Phase 3 Study 4311. The high inter-subject variability and wide therapeutic window (200 to 4000 ng/mL) in maintenance setting can have important implications for therapeutic drug monitoring (TDM). Generally, TDM is generally used for narrow therapeutic index drug. In cases where there is high inter-subject variability, it may be difficult to establish a one-size-fits-all dosing regimen for concizumab. The wide therapeutic window can also affect therapeutic drug monitoring, as it may be more challenging to determine the optimal dosage when there is dose delay or dose interruption. The high inter-subject variability and wide therapeutic window may underscore the importance of individualized dosing and the use of therapeutic drug monitoring to optimize treatment outcomes. However, given the large therapeutic window here, the TDM might be ok to reduce potential outliers, and for patients with low or high exposure, the TDM can help to adjust individual dose according to the maintenance dose setting, resulting in more similar exposure across patients.
L1868: *Although Current PPK model had included nonlinear elimination of concizumab via target mediated drug disposition (TMDD), we noticed several limitations in their PPK model:*
L1869: - *The Current PPK model had some limitations on handling the variabilities: current model tends to put almost all the variability into the residual variabilities with high additive error on log data (SD) = 0.6. This can be problematic because it can make it difficult to determine the true sources of variability. Additionally, this can limit the ability of the model to make accurate predictions.*
- *Additionally, current PPK model fixed the most model structure parameters values for TMDD and PK: only R_{tot} with inter-subject variance is allowed to vary, which can limit the flexibility of the model when analyzing complex PK data with large inter- and intra-subject variability that require more flexibility in the model.*
L1870: *Additional simulation with steady-state exposure estimates for adolescents (12 to 17 years) enrolled in Study 4311 were overlapping with exposure estimates in adults (&gt;18 years) when treated with 0.20 mg/kg concizumab, which indicated no significant effect of age (adolescents [12 to 17 years] versus adults [\ge18 years]).*
L1871: *In summary, PPK modeling and simulation results support no dose adjustment for proposed dose regimen.*
L1872: ## 14.5.2. Exposure-Response Analysis
L1873: The Applicant's exposure-response (E-R) for efficacy analysis of concizumab was conducted with Phase 3 data. The total number of subjects and samples in each exposure-response model was:
L1874: - Free TFPI: 110 subjects with 941 time-matched free TFPI and concizumab plasma concentrations (327 of these samples were below the lower limit of quantification (BLOQ))
- Thrombin peak: 108 subjects with 1048 time-matched thrombin peak and concizumab concentrations
- Bleeding rate model: 117 subjects with 440 bleeding episodes (including data from on-demand treatment

L1881: ## 14.5.2.1. Exposure-Response Analysis of Bleeding Episodes (Repeated Time-to-Event Model)
L1882: An RTTE analysis using a hazard function was performed to analyze the occurrence of treated bleeds.
L1883: The occurrence of bleeding events in hemophilia patients with inhibitors treated on-demand was described by an exponential Gompertz distribution function. The effect of concizumab exposure on the hazard function was evaluated by inclusion of an inhibitory sigmoidal Emax model, parameterized with a constant baseline hazard (h0), maximum reduction in instantaneous bleeding rate from baseline (Emax), plasma concentration to achieve 50% of maximum response (EC50), and Hill coefficient (n).
L1884: ## **Equation 1. Inhibitory Sigmoidal Emax Model for Bleeding**
L1885: h_{(t)} = h_0 \cdot \left(1 - \frac{E_{max} \cdot C^n}{EC_{50}^n \times C^n}\right)
L1886: Exposure-response analysis of bleeding episodes show that the concizumab dosing regimen maintains plasma concentrations at the plateau of effect (Figure 51). The maximum drug effect (Emax) was predicted to be a 93% reduction in annualized bleeding rate (ABR) (Table 116). The exposure-response analysis of bleeding episodes provided an adequate description of the occurrence of the first bleeding episode over time for subjects on concizumab and on-demand as evident from Figure 52.
L1887: **Figure 51. Exposure-response Relationship of Annualized Bleeding Rate**
L1888: The line is the mean model-based prediction. The horizontal light blue line at the bottom represents the median (diamond) and the 5-95% range of the individual average exposure predicted across the trial duration (only including data after restart of Trial 4311), using C_{avg,trial} estimated from the BOV PK model.
L1889: Source: Population PK Study Report Modeling Report – Phase 3a, Figure 7-1.
L1890: Abbreviations: BOV, between-occasion (within-subject) variability; C_{avg,trial}, the average of the individual concentrations predicted across the entire trial duration; PK, pharmacokinetics

L1908: ## 14.5.2.2. Exposure-Response Analysis of Free TFPI
L1909: A sigmoidal Emax model was used to describe concizumab concentration (C) versus free TFPI. The model was parameterized with a baseline (E_0) and a sigmoidal Emax model parameterized with maximum free TFPI response (E_{max}), plasma concentration to achieve 50% of maximum response (EC_{50}), and Hill coefficient (n) according to:
L1910: ## **Equation 2. Sigmoidal Emax model for Free TFPI**
L1911: E = E_0 - E_{max} \times \frac{C^n}{EC_{50}^n \times C^n}
L1912: The exposure metric for the free TFPI exposure-response models was the observed concentrations with time-matched biomarker concentrations.
L1913: All parameter estimates were estimated with acceptable precision, and all BSV parameter estimates had low shrinkage (Table 117). No significant covariates were identified during the SCM procedure (forward inclusion: p&lt;0.01, backward exclusion: p&lt;0.001).

L1938: ## 14.5.2.3. Exposure-Response for Thrombin Peak Model
L1939: A sigmoidal Emax model was used to describe the relationship between concizumab concentration (C) and thrombin peak. The model was parameterized with a baseline (E_0), and a sigmoidal Emax model parameterized with maximum thrombin peak response (E_{max}), plasma concentration to achieve 50% of maximum response (EC_{50}), hill coefficient (n) and a hemophilia B with inhibitors factor on E_0 according to:
L1940: ## Equation 3. Sigmoidal Emax Model for Thrombin Peak
L1941: E = E_0 \cdot COV_{HBwI} + E_{max} \times \frac{C^n}{EC_{50}^n \times C^n}
L1942: The exposure metric for the Thrombin peak exposure-response models was the observed concentrations with time-matched biomarker concentrations. All parameter estimates were estimated with acceptable precision, and all BSV parameter estimates had low shrinkage (Table 118). One significant covariate was identified during the SCM procedure (HBwI was a significant covariate on baseline thrombin peak).
L1943: Exposure-response analysis of thrombin peak shows that the concizumab dosing regimen maintains plasma concentrations at the plateau of effect (Figure 59).

ADD_ERR	Additive error (SD)	nM	19.6	[17.6; 21.6]	8.03
L1951: Rate of successful minimization for bootstrap = 991/1000 samples
L1952: Source: Population PK Study Report Modeling Report – Phase 3a, Table 10-14.
L1953: Abbreviations: E0, baseline peak thrombin; Emax, maximum drug effect; NA, not applicable; SD, standard deviation
L1954: The exposure-response model of thrombin peak showed that at the highest exposure quantile, observed thrombin peak appeared lower than predicted by the model (Figure 59). The model diagnostic plots (Figure 60) support that the model adequately fit the observed data. However, the observed data were well captured by the model when accounting for variability between subjects (see Figure 61).
L1955: Figure 60. Goodness-of-Fit Plots for the Exposure-Response Thrombin Peak
L1956: Data are observed concentrations of thrombin peak versus individual predictions and versus population predictions. Red line indicates loess trend lines of concentration.
L1957: Source: Population PK Study Report Modeling Report – Phase 3a, Figure 10-27.
L1958: Figure 61. VPC of Thrombin Peak Versus Concizumab Concentration
L1959: Visual predictive check of the concizumab concentration-thrombin peak relationship. Data points are time-matched thrombin peak and concizumab concentrations. Solid dark blue line is the median and dashed lines 5th (lower) and 95th (upper) percentiles of the observed data. The corresponding 95% CI for the simulated median (gray) and 5th/95th (blue) percentiles are shown by the coloured ribbons (n=1000) using the final model.
L1960: Source: Population PK Study Report Modeling Report – Phase 3a, Figure 10-26.
L1961: Abbreviations: CI, confidence interval; n, number of subjects; VPC, visual predictive check
L1962: *Reviewer's Comments: The Applicant's exposure response model for bleeding rate, free TFPI, and thrombin peak appears acceptable with an adequate fit of the data as evident. The exposure-response analyses showed that the concizumab dosing regimen maintains plasma concentrations at the plateau of effect, in terms of reducing bleeding rate and free TFPI, and increasing peak thrombin in the thrombin generation assay.*
L1963: Overall, exposure-response analyses support the proposed dosing regimen of concizumab.

b) Week 0 is the time of trial restart
c) Visit will take place after the concizumab exposure levels are available according to in Section 9.2.2
d) Only patients enrolled after the treatment pause will sign the Informed Consent at visit 1a
e) Only patients enrolled in the trial before the treatment pause and allocated to arm 2, 3 and 4, will re-consent at Visit 2a
f) Only patients enrolled in the trial before the treatment pause and randomised to arm 1, will re-consent at visit 9a
g) Only applicable for arm 1 patients, who are receiving concizumab at Visit 9a
h) Only applicable for patients in arm 2, 3 and 4, who are receiving concizumab at visit 2a
i) Only applicable for patients enrolled in the trial before treatment pause. Site must ensure patients do not violate discontinuation criteria according to Section 8.1 before reinitiating dosing with concizumab
j) Patients randomised to arm 2 before the treatment pause will not be re-randomised at visit 2a
k) Weight only, except at visit 1 where height is measured
l) For patients having concizumab dosing at visit 2a there will be a 24-hour PK session at visit 2a and 9a. Please see details for PK sampling in Section 2.1. For on-demand patients (arm 1) only the pre-dose sample will be taken
m) Sample is taken pre-dose. Patients must not inject trial medication on this visit day before the sample is taken
n) Concizumab plasma concentration for dose adjustment (visit 4a for arm 2-4 and visit 9a.2 for arm 1) will be analysed using the concizumab ELISA in vitro diagnostic (IVD) device
o) Only one sample to be taken, either factor VIII for HA patients or factor IX for HB patients
p) Only applicable for patients having site visit due to dose-escalation
q) Only for patients above \ge 17 years old (at visit 1a)
r) Only applicable for patients enrolled in the trial before the treatment pause
s) Site visit must be performed in case of dose escalation. Phone visit is allowed for patients having dose de-escalation or dose unchanged. See section 9.2.2 for further details
t) A separate Informed Consent Form must be signed before any samples are taken. See section 9.7 for further details
u) Whole blood for DNA is only taken at screening
v) See Section 9.2.5 for further details

L2006: ## 22. Data Integrity-Related Consults (Office of Scientific Investigations, Other Inspections)
L2007: A clinical inspection summary was completed by Dr. Anthony Orenzia in the Office of Scientific Investigations, final signature date March 2, 2023. Two foreign clinical study sites and the Applicant's site were selected for evaluation as these sites had the highest patient enrollment (Krakow n = 5 patients and Zagreb n = 4 patients) and overall, a low proportion of patients were enrolled in the US (6/118 patients (5.1%)). Enrollment in other countries in the European Union was higher (e.g., Ukraine 10/118 patients (8.5%)), however due to the ongoing war in Ukraine inspections in these countries were not conducted.
L2008: Dr. Orenzia states in the review regarding the study site of Dr. Joanna Zdziarska/ Site 480 (ul. Mikołaja Kopernika 17, Krakow, Małopolskie, 31-501, Poland) that a total of six study subjects were screened (one subject was a rescreen); five study subjects were enrolled. Three of the five enrolled subjects completed the study. Two subjects who discontinued were transferred to alternative therapies during the study pause. The study was ongoing at the time of the inspection. Regulatory and source documents were reviewed. The audit involved a review of records and procedures related to the clinical study protocol and its amendments; subject selection criteria and consenting; test article controls, including accountability; source data evaluation, including primary efficacy endpoint of bleeding events; adverse event reporting, including assessment of
L2009: under-reporting of serious adverse events, and laboratory testing. Source records for all enrolled study subjects were examined and verifiable. Records were noted to be complete, legible, and organized. Study activities were conducted in compliance with the protocol. The primary efficacy endpoint was verifiable. There was no evidence of under-reporting of adverse events (e.g., no lapses in approvals or failure to file required reports) were noted) and no discrepancies were noted. No significant objectionable findings were found during the inspection. A list of inspectional observations (Form FDA 483) was not issued by FDA at the close-out of the inspection.
L2010: Dr. Orenzia states in his review regarding the study site of Dr. Ana Boban/Site 385 (Kispaticeva 12, University Hospital Centre, Zagreb 10000 HRV, Croatia) that five study subjects were screened; four study subjects were enrolled and randomized. Three subjects discontinued (withdrew from the study). A single subject was still on treatment during the inspection. Ethics committee approvals, study correspondence, drug accountability, facility adequacy, staff qualifications, and monitoring procedures were reviewed. This inspection covered the safety of study subjects along with serious adverse event reporting, protocol deviations, subject eligibility, overall protocol compliance, and the verification of source documentation related to study endpoint criteria. Adverse event and serious adverse reporting appeared adequate and consistent with the study protocol requirements and the case report form reports. The primary efficacy endpoint (bleeding events) was verifiable. There was no evidence of under-reporting of adverse events (e.g., no lapses in approvals or failure to file required reports were noted). No list of inspectional observations (FDA Form 483) was issued by FDA at the close-out of the site inspection.
L2011: Dr. Orenzia states in his review regarding the study site of Novo Nordisk Inc./Applicant (800 Scudders Mill Rd., Plainsboro, NJ 08536) that the Applicant inspection involved comprehensive onsite review of study monitor selection, site and clinical investigator selection, clinical site monitoring, form FDA 1572s, financial disclosures, safety reporting and handling, data monitoring committee activities, study documents, standard operating procedures, data collection and handling, protocol deviations, and investigational product disposition. Monitoring actions taken for those clinical investigators who did not comply with the investigational plan appeared to be adequate. Procedure documents for Novo Nordisk's review and processing of serious adverse events (SAEs), such as (a) Q040018 Handling Individual Safety Cases in Argus, (b) Q140826 NNI Product Safety GxP Instructions, and (c) Q148963 NNI Electronic Submission of Expedited Safety Reports were assessed during the inspections. FDA's inspection also assessed safety oversight performed by medical monitors and safety advisors in addition to the data monitoring committee (DMC). No discrepancies were observed for adverse event reporting. At the end of the Applicant inspection, no FDA Form 483 was issued. However, some of the following relevant discussion items at the close out meeting with Applicant deserve mention: (a) Ensure adequate oversight of drug product disposition at clinical sites to track and to evaluate investigative drug products, for example, with temperature deviations, prior to subject dispensing. Sites had no additional checkpoints preventing dispensing of the investigative drug product after initial assignment by interactive web response systems. For example, the audit found that the investigational drug product was administered to Subject (b) (6) (treatment arm not specified) after undergoing a temperature deviation at the clinical site. However, no consequential adverse events were reported following temperature excursions, and (b) Complete query resolution and clarifications between affiliates (that is, affiliate study managers and
L2012: monitors) and clinical investigators should be documented allowing for appropriate handling and follow-up. For example, clarification received from a clinical investigator, for the timely reporting of the onset dates of a serious adverse event via e-mail, was not captured in the queries or updated in the electronic data capture data. In general, the Applicant's oversight and monitoring of this clinical study appear to be acceptable.
L2013: Dr. Orencia concludes in his review that based on the inspections, the study appears to have been conducted adequately and the study data derived from these clinical investigator sites are considered reliable. The data from Study NN7415-4311 submitted to the Agency for assessment appear acceptable in support of the proposed indication.
L2014: *Clinical Reviewer Comment: The clinical reviewer agrees with the conclusions in Dr. Orencia's review final signature date March 2, 2023. No additional clinical study site inspections are recommended at this time for Study 4311.*

L2064: Missed 1 dose(s); resume with a single dose
Concizumab (ng/mL)
Time since first missed dose (days)
Figure 1: Simulation of concizumab plasma concentration when treatment is resumed with a single maintenance dose after 1 missed dose. The graph shows Concizumab concentration (ng/mL) on the Y-axis (log scale, 50 to 10000) versus Time since first missed dose (days) on the X-axis (-5 to 30). The concentration drops sharply after the missed dose (around day 0) and then recovers slowly, reaching a steady state around 200 ng/mL after approximately 30 days. The shaded area indicates the therapeutic window.
L2065: Missed 2 dose(s); resume with a double dose
Missed 6 dose(s); resume with a double dose
Concizumab (ng/mL)
Time since first missed dose (days)
Figure 2: Simulation of concizumab plasma concentration when treatment is resumed with a double maintenance dose after 2 (left) or 6 (right) missed doses. Both graphs show Concizumab concentration (ng/mL) on the Y-axis (log scale, 50 to 10000) versus Time since first missed dose (days) on the X-axis (-5 to 30). The left graph (2 missed doses) shows the concentration dropping sharply and recovering quickly, reaching a steady state around 200 ng/mL within 10 days. The right graph (6 missed doses) shows the concentration dropping sharply and recovering quickly, reaching a steady state around 200 ng/mL within 10 days. The shaded area indicates the therapeutic window.

L2068: Diagram illustrating the mechanism of action of Concizumab. Concizumab binds to TFPI, preventing it from inhibiting Factor Xa (Fxa). This allows the Fxa produced by the FVIIa/Tissue Factor (TF) complex to proceed, leading to the activation of Factor II (Prothrombin) to Factor IIa (Thrombin), which ultimately results in a Blood clot.
L2069: Figure 2 is a line graph showing Concizumab concentration (ng/mL) on the Y-axis (log scale, 10 to 20000) versus Nominal time after entering phase 3 (weeks) on the X-axis (0 to 24 weeks). The graph compares patients with Thrombotic Events (TE, solid lines) and those without (No TE, dashed lines). Three specific cases (TE Case 1, TE Case 2, TE Case 3) are highlighted. TE Case 1 and TE Case 3 show high peak concentrations (around 10000 ng/mL) shortly after the loading dose (around 4 weeks), followed by a sharp decline. TE Case 2 shows a lower peak concentration (around 2000 ng/mL) and a slower decline. The No TE group generally maintains lower concentrations, mostly below 2000 ng/mL.
L2070: Figure 3 displays two simulated concentration-time profiles (A and B) of Concizumab (ng/mL) versus Time (weeks) following a loading dose of 1 mg/kg and an initial daily dose of 0.2 mg/kg. The Y-axis ranges from 50 to 10,000 ng/mL, and the X-axis ranges from 0 to 40 weeks.
Panel A (Without Dose Adjustment): The concentration rises rapidly after the loading dose, reaching a steady-state level around 700 ng/mL by Week 10. The shaded area represents the 90% prediction interval, which is wide, spanning from approximately 200 ng/mL to 10,000 ng/mL. The dark line represents the median exposure level. The trough concentration (Ctrough) is around 200 ng/mL.
Panel B (With Dose Adjustment): The concentration rises rapidly after the loading dose, reaching a steady-state level around 700 ng/mL by Week 10. The shaded area represents the 90% prediction interval, which is significantly narrower, spanning from approximately 500 ng/mL to 5,000 ng/mL. The dark line represents the median exposure level. The trough concentration (Ctrough) is around 500 ng/mL, indicating that the dose adjustment successfully narrowed the exposure range to the target range.
L2071: Figure 4: Concizumab PK Following 0.25 mg/kg Daily Dose With or Without a Loading Dose of 1 mg/kg (Simulation). Two scenarios are compared: 1 mg/kg loading dose (dark line) and No loading dose (light line). Both scenarios show steady-state concentrations reached by day 6. The 1 mg/kg loading dose results in significantly higher steady-state concentrations (around 500 ng/mL) compared to the No loading dose (around 200 ng/mL). Error bars are included for both data sets.
L2072: Figure 5: Exposure-Response Relationship of Annualized Bleeding Rate. The curve shows a steep decrease in annualized bleeding rate as Concizumab concentration increases, leveling off around 1 bleed/year at high concentrations. A horizontal light blue line at the bottom represents the median (diamond) and the 5-95% range of the individual average exposure predicted across the study duration, using C_avg, study estimated from the BOV PK model.
L2073: Figure 6: Exposure-response Relationships for Free TFPI and Thrombin Peak as Determined by Exposure-Response Models, Study 4311. L2075: Figure 8 is a schematic diagram illustrating the Concizumab Dosing regimen. The regimen starts with a Loading Dose (LD) of 1 mg/kg. This is followed by an initial dose of 0.20 mg/kg. The dose adjustment occurs during the LD dose adjustment period (V4a or 9a.2). The adjustment leads to three potential individual maintenance doses (V4a.1 or 9a.3): 0.25 mg/kg, 0.20 mg/kg, or 0.15 mg/kg. The decision for dose adjustment is based on concizumab exposure measured at visit 4a or 9a.2 (after 4 weeks of concizumab PPX):
A) If the concizumab exposure measured at visit 4a or 9a.2 (after 4 weeks of concizumab PPX) is <200 ng/mL and there are no safety concerns as judged by the Investigator after considerations on available laboratory parameters, clinical picture and medical history of the patient, the dose is increased to 0.25 mg/kg.
B) If the concizumab exposure measured at visit 4a or 9a.2 (after 4 weeks of concizumab PPX) is >4000 ng/mL, the dose is decreased to 0.15 mg/kg.

L2086: Figure 18 displays two graphs illustrating thrombin peak (nM) versus concizumab concentration (ng/mL).

L2101: Flowchart illustrating the Study Scheme, Study 4311, After Restart. The study is divided into four phases: Screening, Main part (24/32 weeks), Extension part, and Follow-up.
Screening: Includes three patient groups: Patient on OnD treatment, Patients transferred from explorer4, and Patients on PPX and additional patients on OnD treatment. The screening period is 3 weeks (V1a to V2a).
Main part (24/32 weeks): This phase is divided into a Dose setting period (Up to 8 weeks) and a treatment period (At least 24 weeks).
Arm 1 (No PPX (OnD)): Continues treatment.
Arm 2 (Concizumab PPX (maintenance dose)a): Starts treatment at Visit 2a.
Arm 3 (Concizumab PPX (maintenance dose)a): Starts treatment at Visit 3.
Arm 4 (Concizumab PPX (maintenance dose)a): Starts treatment at Visit 4.
Extension part: All patients receiving Concizumab PPX continue treatment (Concizumab PPX (maintenance dose)a) for 128-136 weeks (V9a/V10a to V26a).
Follow-up: Patients receiving Concizumab PPX continue treatment (Concizumab PPX (maintenance dose)a) for 7 weeks (V26a to V27a). Patients receiving No concizumab PPX continue treatment (No concizumab PPX) for 7 weeks (V26a to V27a).

L2111: Figure 43 displays six goodness-of-fit plots for the Final Population PK Model for Concizumab. The plots are arranged in a 2x3 grid:

Middle Left: Weighted Residual vs Population predicted Concentration (value). The residuals are centered around zero, showing no systematic pattern.
Middle Right: Weighted Residual vs Time (h). The residuals are centered around zero, showing no systematic pattern over time.
Bottom Left: Weighted Residual vs Theoretical Quantiles. The data points closely follow the diagonal line, indicating a good fit for the weighted residuals.

L2119: Figure 51: Exposure-response Relationship of Annualized Bleeding Rate. The graph plots Annualized bleeding rate (bleeds/year) on the Y-axis (ranging from 0 to 20) against Concizumab concentration (ng/mL) on the X-axis (ranging from 0 to 5000). The curve shows a steep decrease in annualized bleeding rate as concizumab concentration increases, leveling off at a low rate (around 1 bleed/year) at high concentrations. L2120: Figure 52 displays two Kaplan-Meier Visual Predictive Checks (VPCs) comparing observed survival (blue line) against model predictions (green shaded area) for the occurrence of the first bleeding episode over time (h).
Panel A shows the VPC for subjects on Concizumab Treatment. The observed survival curve (blue line) decreases steadily, starting near 100% survival at Time (h) = 0 and reaching approximately 40% survival by Time (h) = 10,000. The green shaded area represents the 95% interval for model predictions, which closely envelops the observed survival curve.
Panel B shows the VPC for subjects on On-Demand Treatment. The observed survival curve (blue line) decreases rapidly, starting near 100% survival at Time (h) = 0 and dropping below 20% survival by Time (h) = 2,000. The green shaded area represents the 95% interval for model predictions, which also closely envelops the observed survival curve.
Vertical black lines across the blue line in both panels indicate right censoring of subject observations.
L2121: Figure 53 displays four Kaplan-Meier Visual Predictive Checks (VPCs) showing the survival percentage of subjects over time (in hours) for the occurrence of the 1st, 2nd, 3rd, and 4th bleeding episodes (Events 1, 2, 3, and 4) for subjects on Concizumab. The Y-axis represents Survival (%) and the X-axis represents Time (h), ranging from 0 to 10,000 hours.
In all four plots, the blue line represents the observed percentage of subjects without bleeding events over time. The green shaded area indicates the 95% interval for model predictions of bleeding events over time. Vertical black lines crossing the blue line indicate right censoring of subject observations.
The plots show that the survival percentage decreases over time, indicating an increasing risk of subsequent bleeding episodes. The survival rate is highest for Event 1 (around 40% survival at 10,000 hours) and lowest for Event 4 (around 20% survival at 10,000 hours).
